===== Theorem 1.1 HIT 1 AT 4204 =====
Yang–Mills Existence, Mass Gap, and Nontriviality for Fixed Compact Simple Gauge Groups via Karcher Blocking and Exact Haar–Pivot Matching Harold D. Kirk III1,2,∗ 1 University of Memphis, Memphis, Tennessee 38152, USA 2 ORCID: 0009-0000-6014-8451 August 3, 2026 Abstract Let G be a fixed compact connected simple Lie group and let ρ be a fixed faithful finite-dimensional unitary representation. We construct four-dimensional pure G Yang–Mills theory as a zero-spacing, infinite-volume limit of reflection-positive Wilson lattice measures. The Wilson plaquette function is normalized at the identity to the background quadratic form used to define the coupling u = g2 . A weighted forty-nine-path Karcher block, complete sixteen-alias normal section, charged chart- complement partition, and permanent rooted domain produce a uniform scale-two map on a centered invariant graph, u(2L) = u(L) + 11 CA(G) log 2 24π2 u(L)2 + OG(u(L)3 ). The complete linear block certificate is independent of color and tensors with Ig; all nonlinear constants are finite and may depend on G. At the physical handoff, a group-dependent product Sobolev order 2sG > dG/2 gives a Peter–Weyl Fourier–Wiener landing for the exact one-pivot and multipivot densities. Separated Haar pivots contract nonvacuum compact flux, optical covariance contracts normal-field stretches, and all remaining vertices retain their support charge. A single marked replica–BKAR forest then gives a boundary-to-center two-root connector and one physical clustering rate. General invariant-jet transport, displacement and stress Ward identities, finite dimension-four mixing, and a dimension-six rotation regulator yield a tempered, reflection-positive, translation-continuous, O(4)-covariant Schwinger family. Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction gives a positive-energy Poincaré-covariant local theory with unique vacuum and a strictly positive Hamiltonian gap. Endpoint-safe curvature-source transport produces a nonzero local vector with positive spectral mass in a finite interval above the gap. The theorem concerns the local gauge-invariant curvature-jet observable theory. No estimate is asserted uniformly over the class of compact simple groups. ∗hkirk@memphis.edu 1 Contents 1 The compact-simple-group theorem and proof boundary 3 2 Fixed-group Wilson geometry and Karcher blocking 5 3 Charged chart complement and irregular sector 10 4 Permanent weak renormalization group 23 5 Invariant graph and universal coefficient 39 6 Directed handoff and higher-order Peter–Weyl landing 52 7 Compact boundary-to-center closure 71 8 General invariant jets and Ward identities 72 9 Continuum reconstruction and spectral gap 89 10 Nontriviality and finite spectral window 90 11 Completion for arbitrary fixed compact simple gauge groups 94 A Endpoint packet-shell verification 94 B Root data and representation normalization 109 C The fixed-group Sobolev and Peter–Weyl ledger 110 D Ordinary periodic torons, seams, and wrapping templates 110 E Invariant tensor spaces through engineering dimension six 111 F Theorem-level specialization and dependency map 112 G Representative fixed-group certifications 113 2 1 The compact-simple-group theorem and proof boundary 1.1 Problem and result The constructive Yang–Mills problem asks for a nontrivial quantum gauge field theory on R4 satisfying the Osterwalder–Schrader and Wightman requirements and possessing a strictly positive mass gap [1]. The physical scale, thermodynamic limit, zero lattice spacing, Euclidean covariance, local spectral content, and non-abelian field geometry must be controlled in one compatible construction. Fix throughout a compact connected simple Lie group G, a faithful unitary representation ρ : G → U(Vρ) of dimension dρ, and a positive normalization of the invariant quadratic form for which the adjoint color factor is denoted CA(G). The Wilson plaquette function is wG,ρ(U) = cρ  1 − 1 dρ Re Tr ρ(U)  , where cρ > 0 is chosen once so that the Hessian at the identity is the quadratic form used by the background-field coupling coordinate u = g2 . This choice is a finite linear normalization of the bare Wilson coupling and is part of the fixed-group input register. Theorem 1.1 (Four-dimensional Yang–Mills construction for fixed compact simple G). For every fixed pair (G, ρ) as above, there is a directed sequence of reflection-positive Wilson lattice measures, with microscopic spacing tending to zero and periodic volume tending to infinity, whose local gauge-invariant curvature-jet Schwinger functions converge to a nontrivial Euclidean field theory on R4 . The limiting Schwinger family is tempered, permutation symmetric, reflection positive, translation continuous, exponentially clustering, and O(4) covariant. Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction yields a positive-energy Poincaré-covariant local theory with a unique vacuum and a number m⋆(G) > 0 such that H Ω⊥ ≥ m⋆(G). On the centered invariant weak trajectory, the exact scale-two coupling map is u(2L) = u(L) + 11 CA(G) log 2 24π2 u(L)2 + OG(u(L)3 ), with uniform volume and cutoff control in the permanent weak regime. The reconstructed theory contains a nonzero reflected local curvature vector and positive spectral mass in a finite interval [m⋆(G), M⋆(G)]. 1.2 Fixed-group conventions and finite register Write g = Lie(G) and dG = dim G. All Haar measures are normalized. The finite-volume approximants use the ordinary periodic link space GE(Λ) with periodic gauge transformations; no twisted boundary condition or nontrivial finite-volume bundle sector is summed. This is sufficient for the claimed local theory on the contractible space R4 . The bi-invariant metric, exponential coordinates, Laplacian ∆G, Casimir eigenvalues, and operator norms are those determined by the declared quadratic form. In curvature formulas, Tr(XY ) denotes this normalized invariant bilinear pairing; raw representation traces are written Tr ρ(·). Let sG :=  dG 4  + 1, 2sG > dG 2 . The fixed-group constant register CG consists of the finite quantities used below: the injectivity and convexity radii, a positive local Karcher radius, finite holomorphic BCH and logarithm bounds through order 2sG + 3, the faithful-action Hessian and complement charge, the Peter–Weyl constant, the finite ordinary-periodic toron/seam template register, the adjoint color factor, and the finite invariant-jet dimensions through the retained degree. Every statement called uniform is uniform in cutoff, volume, directed depth, periodic wrapping, admitted exterior histories, and the finite reflected template family, but its constants may depend on CG. Proposition 1.2 (Reflection-positive Wilson input). For every finite periodic lattice Λ and u > 0, in the ordinary periodic link sector, dµG,ρ Λ,u(U) = Z−1 Λ,u exp " −u−1 X p wG,ρ(Up) # Y e dmG(Ue) is gauge invariant and reflection positive for coordinate reflections. Moreover wG,ρ ≥ 0, its zero set is the identity, and on one fixed identity neighborhood cG∥X∥2 ≤ wG,ρ(eX ) ≤ CG∥X∥2 . 3 Proof. The normalized character satisfies Re Tr ρ(U) ≤ dρ, with equality only when ρ(U) = I; faithfulness gives U = e. The Hessian statement follows from the chosen normalization and compactness. Up to a field- independent constant, each plaquette factor is exp{t(χρ +χρ)} with t > 0. Its power series is a nonnegative linear combination of characters of tensor products of ρ and ρ̄, and their irreducible decompositions have nonnegative integer multiplicities. The standard character-expansion reflection-positivity argument therefore applies. 1.3 Proof architecture The proof has four stages. First, the exact Karcher block is placed in a complete full-alias normal chart and combined with a charged chart-complement partition, permanent analytic domain, marked connected logarithm, Wick-stable jet block, and centered invariant graph. The folded spacetime symbol tensors with Ig, while all nonlinear estimates are admitted by the finite register CG. Second, exact background-scheme conjugacy identifies the universal marginal coefficient and a directed schedule reaches the compact matching scale. The higher product Sobolev operator Q p(1−∆p)sG replaces the rank-one order and supplies the fixed-G Fourier–Wiener rows. Third, complete Haar and optical variables are kept in one partition function. A mixed replica– BKAR forest makes every root-to-root path pay separated Haar contraction, optical covariance decay, or pre-existing support charge. Ordinary-periodic toron backgrounds, seams, reflections, and wrapping certificates enter only through the finite template theorem of Appendix D. Finally, a least-label source forest transports the complete invariant-jet system. The degree-four block is group-independent because a compact simple Lie algebra has a unique invariant symmetric bilinear form; possible cubic color tensors first enter the finite dimension-six block. Ward identities, rotation-defect estimates, reflection-positive recombination, and Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction give covariance, the unique vacuum, the gap, and noncollapse. 1.4 External inputs and exact proof boundary Only the theorem-level inputs in Table 1 are imported. The fixed-group geometry, action charge, higher Sobolev landing, periodic-toron template theorem, invariant-jet admission, matching constants, and noncollapse source are internal. Unit-tangent scheme identification and control of the flat complement. 1.5 Self-contained relation to the rank-one construction The companion SU(2) manuscript [14] is a completely expanded rank-one specialization and a useful independent check. It is not a logical input to Theorem 1.1: every common analytic theorem used here is stated and proved in this paper, and every new fixed-group admission is printed below. Appendix F gives a theorem-level specialization map so that a referee can identify which arguments are color-free, which tensor with Ig, and which require new group data. 1.6 Scope The theorem concerns the local gauge-invariant curvature-jet observable sector for each fixed compact connected simple G. It does not assert a bound uniform over groups, a simultaneous large-rank limit, control of every extended Wilson-line or generalized-symmetry sector, or uniqueness of the continuum limit outside the directed construction. Quotient groups are included through their actual compact link group. The construction uses only the ordinary periodic finite-volume sector and retains all flat toron backgrounds occurring in that sector; no claim about a sum over twisted boundary conditions is made or needed. 2 Fixed-group Wilson geometry and Karcher blocking Let Λ be a finite periodic four-dimensional lattice and use the reflection-positive Wilson measure of Proposition 1.2. For each oriented retained link, use the straight two-link path with weight 7/8 and the forty-eight signed-corner staples with weight 1/384 each. On a fixed strongly convex identity tube, define the retained link as their weighted Karcher mean in the bi-invariant metric. Lemma 2.1 (Fixed-group convex Karcher and holomorphic atlas). There are radii 0 < r0,G < r1,G such that the squared-distance functions, product, inverse, exponential, logarithm, and weighted Karcher critical point extend holomorphically to the corresponding local complexification tube. The Karcher Hessian is bounded below there by a positive constant depending only on G. All derivatives through arbitrary prescribed finite order are bounded on the smaller tube. Proof. A compact Lie group with a bi-invariant analytic metric has positive injectivity and strong-convexity radii. The sectional curvature is bounded because G is compact, and the Hessian comparison theorem gives a positive lower Hessian bound on a sufficiently small ball. In a faithful matrix representation the local complexification is an open neighborhood in the complexified group; multiplication, inversion, exponential, and the principal logarithm are holomorphic there. The analytic implicit-function theorem applied to the Karcher critical equation gives the holomorphic mean. Cauchy estimates on nested compact tubes give every fixed derivative order; standard compact Lie-group analytic facts are summarized in [15]. Proposition 2.2 (Exact block covariance and local submersion). The retained map is gauge covariant, orientation covariant, signed-hypercubic covariant, and aligned-reflection covariant. Its linearization at the flat connection has full retained rank. On one fixed weak tube it admits a finite-volume analytic normal section whose matrix kernel and first three marked derivatives are exponentially local, uniformly in volume. Proof. Every path has the same endpoints, and the weighted squared-distance functional is invariant under simultaneous left and right multiplication. Uniqueness of the Karcher minimizer gives gauge and orientation covariance. The signed-corner path family is invariant under the lattice symmetry group. At the flat point, the exact Fourier symbol is the color-free spacetime symbol tensored with Ig and has acoustic value 2I ⊗ Ig and a uniformly gapped normal frame. The full-alias section and normal-fiber contraction are proved in the later normal-chart chapters. Proposition 2.3 (Exact coarea–fiber identity). At finite volume, in the normal fiber chart, dU = J0 det(I + DN P)−1 dW dζ, 5 where the determinant is the ordinary finite-dimensional determinant. The fixed linear determinant is owned once as a scalar normalization, and the field-dependent logarithm is one connected analytic activity. 2.1 Full-alias normal right inverse Theorem 2.4 (Complete folded symbol and flat normal right inverse). Write qe = k + πe, e ∈ {0, 1}4 , and decompose the one-alias symbol as M(q) = ϑS(q) + (1 − ϑ)C(q), ϑ = 7 8 , where Sµν(q) = 2δµν cos qµ 2 , Cµµ(q) = 2 cos qµ 2 Y λ̸=µ cos qλ, and, for µ ̸= ν and {α, β} = {0, 1, 2, 3} \ {µ, ν}, Cµν(q) = 1 3 sin qµ sin qν 2 2 cos qα cos qβ + cos qα + cos qβ + 2  . Analyticity in a fixed strip gives exponential Fourier-kernel decay. 2.2 Exact normal fiber chart and coarea Let Xm, Ym be exponentially weighted Banach coordinate spaces and let L : Xm → Ym, P : Ym → Xm, LP = I, ∥P∥m = p < 1. Put K = ker L and write B(x) = Lx + N(x), N(0) = 0, DN(0) = 0. 7 Theorem 2.5 (Normal fiber chart). For w ∈ Ym and ζ ∈ K, consider F(y; w, ζ) = y + N(Py + ζ) − w. Assume on a product ball that η := sup ∥DN(Py + ζ)P∥m < 1. Then there is a unique analytic solution y = T(w, ζ) and PN (w, ζ) := PT(w, ζ) + ζ is an exact fiber chart: B(PN (w, ζ)) = w. If A = I + DN(PN )P, then DwT = A− 1, DζT = −A− 1DN(PN )|K, and hence DwPN = PA− 1, DζPN = IK − PA− 1DN(PN )|K. All derivatives through order three are finite sums of products of A− 1, P, D2 N, D3 N and are exponentially local in the same Banach matrix algebra. Proof. For fixed (w, ζ), the map y 7→ w − N(Py + ζ) is an η-contraction on a sufficiently chosen invariant ball. Banach’s fixed-point theorem gives T. The exact fiber identity follows by substitution. Differentiating T + N(PT + ζ) = w gives the displayed first derivatives. Repeated differentiation gives the second- and third-order Bell- polynomial formulas. Since ∥A− 1∥ ≤ (1 − η)− 1, every term is bounded in the same exponential matrix algebra. Corollary 2.6 (Explicit zero-detail constants). Let Mr(ρ) = sup ∥x∥m≤ρ ∥Dr N(x)∥m, r = 2, 3, and suppose pρM2(ρ) ≤ 1 4 . Theorem 2.7 (Exact coarea factor). Let J0(y, ζ) = Py + ζ. Relative to this fixed linear frame, det DPN = det J0 det I + DN(PN )P −1 . Thus the field-dependent coarea logarithm is − log det I + DN(PN )P  , while det J0 is a once-only field-independent normalization. Proof. The chart factors as (w, ζ) 7→ (T(w, ζ), ζ) 7→ J0(T, ζ). The first derivative is block triangular:  A− 1 −A− 1DN|K 0 IK  . Its determinant is det A− 1. 8 2.3 Nonlinear forty-nine-path section Lemma 2.8 (Finite-cell analytic atlas and uniform Cauchy bounds). Let P49 be the finite path atlas for one retained cell and its finite aligned-reflection template family. There exist fixed radii 0 < η0 < η1, uniform under the standing convention, such that the product exponential, multiplication, inversion, logarithm, and Karcher-mean maps defining every template extend holomorphically from Tη0 to Tη1 . Write the canonically rescaled local block as B(x) = Lx + N(x), N(0) = 0, DN(0) = 0. For every exponential kernel rate 0 < m < mP and r = 2, 3, there is a finite constant CN,r(m; η0, η1) < ∞ such that sup x∈Tη0 ∥Dr N(x)∥Km ≤ CN,r(m; η0, η1), uniformly under the standing convention. The same constants, enlarged by a finite maximum, serve the aligned-reflection templates. Proof. For one retained cell the block depends on finitely many g coordinates. The exponential and multiplication maps are entire, while inversion and the principal logarithm are holomorphic on a fixed neighborhood of the identity. At the flat field the Karcher critical point has positive Hessian on the retained tangent space; the analytic implicit-function theorem therefore supplies a unique holomorphic Karcher mean on a fixed complex neighborhood. The path family and the reflected template family are finite, so one may choose common nested radii 0 < η0 < η1 for all of them. On the larger finite-dimensional complex polydisc, every elementary map and their finite compositions are holomorphic and uniformly bounded. Cauchy’s integral estimate on the smaller polydisc gives uniform derivative bounds through order three with denominator powers of η1 − η0. The global block is obtained by translating this fixed finite cell. Each output row meets only a fixed number of input cells, and each input column enters only a fixed number of output cells. Multiplying the finite-cell Cauchy bounds by these fixed incidence numbers gives the two-sided exponentially weighted Schur-kernel bounds. These incidence numbers do not depend on the total volume or preceding weak depth. The aligned-reflection family is finite, so one takes a finite maximum. Proposition 2.9 (Model-specific nonlinear normal section for the forty-nine-path block). Let pm := ∥P0∥Km < 0.546, C2 := CN,2(m; η0, η1), and choose ρm = min  η0 4 , 1 4pmC2  . For every retained coordinate w with ∥w∥Km ≤ ρm, the retained-space equation y + N(P0y) = w has a unique solution y = T(w, 0) in ∥y∥Km ≤ 2ρm. The zero-detail normal section S(w) := P0T(w, 0) satisfies B(S(w)) = w, ∥S(w)∥Km ≤ 2pmρm. It is analytic and exponentially quasi-local, and its first three marked derivatives have volume- and depth-independent bounds. The same conclusion holds on the aligned-reflection templates after taking the minimum radius over that finite family. Proof. For fixed w, define Φw(y) := w − N(P0y). If ∥y∥ ≤ 2ρm, then ∥P0y∥ ≤ η0/2. Since DN(0) = 0, Lemma 2.8 gives ∥DyΦw(y)∥ = ∥DN(P0y)P0∥ ≤ 2p2 mC2ρm ≤ pm 2 < 1 2 . 9 The second-order integral formula gives ∥N(P0y)∥ ≤ 1 2 C2∥P0y∥2 ≤ 2C2p2 mρ2 m ≤ pm 2 ρm < ρm. Thus Φw maps the 2ρm ball into itself and is a contraction. For its fixed point, B(P0T) = LP0T + N(P0T) = T + N(P0T) = w. Analyticity follows from the analytic implicit-function theorem. Differentiation through order three gives finite Bell polynomials in (I + DN(S)P0)−1 , P0, D2 N, D3 N. The inverse norm is below two and the other factors are bounded by Theorem 2.4 and Lemma 2.8. 3 Charged chart complement and irregular sector Put g = √ u and Rg = g−2ε with 0 < ε < 1/6. The symbol χ > 0 denotes the curvature-bad threshold in this section and in the following two irregular-sector sections; the rooted support exponent remains denoted by κ elsewhere. For an enlarged finite tube cell Q+ define the faithful fine Wilson charge and retained coherent deficit by the same representation-normalized plaquette function used in the microscopic measure: AQ+ (U) = X p⊂Q+ wG,ρ(Up), DQ(U) = X p′⊂Q′ wG,ρ(Wp′ ). Let TQ be a fixed smooth invariant dispersion function formed from the squared relative-loop distances of the forty-nine path inputs. It vanishes on the flat set, its small sublevel set lies inside the common Karcher chart, and it uses only one fixed enlarged-cell template. On that sublevel set the retained holonomies Wp′ are defined by the local Karcher atlas. Multiply the displayed retained deficit by a fixed chart cutoff that is one on a prescribed smaller Karcher sublevel and zero before the edge of the larger Karcher tube; this gives a smooth invariant extension to the complete finite-cell configuration space. The threshold tch below is chosen so that the entire curvature-label support lies where this cutoff equals one. Lemma 3.1 (Representation-normalized charge and derivative ledger). For every fixed G, faithful ρ, and integer 0 ≤ r ≤ 2sG + 3, there are finite constants depending only on the fixed cell atlas such that wG,ρ(U) ≥ 0, wG,ρ(U) = 0 ⇐⇒ U = e, cG∥X∥2 ≤ wG,ρ(eX ) ≤ CG∥X∥2 (∥X∥ ≤ r0,G), and ∥DwG,ρ(U)∥ ≤ CGwG,ρ(U)1/2 , ∥Dr wG,ρ(U)∥ ≤ CG,r (2 ≤ r ≤ 2sG + 3). The same bounds, with finite template constants, hold after composition with every Wilson word, retained plaquette word, reflected word, or seam word used below. Proof. Nonnegativity and the zero set are Proposition 1.2. In the chosen invariant metric the Hessian of wG,ρ at the identity is positive definite; Taylor’s theorem gives the two-sided quadratic comparison. The first-derivative estimate follows from that comparison on the identity tube. On its compact complement wG,ρ has a positive minimum while its derivative is bounded, so the same inequality holds globally after enlarging the constant. All higher derivatives are bounded because wG,ρ is a smooth matrix coefficient on compact G. Every word in the fixed path atlas is a finite composition of multiplication and inversion, so the chain rule gives the asserted finite derivative bounds. Lemma 3.2 (Simultaneous selected-anchor derivative). Select the first fine link of every straight two-link anchor as the anchor variable; these links are distinct. Hold every remaining fine link in a detail list Z, and write the simultaneous retained-coordinate map in product exponential charts as b BZ : a 7−→ w. On the common weak chart, D b BZ(a) = ϑ(I + KZ(a)), ϑ = 7 8 , 10 where KZ has fixed propagation range and sup a max{∥KZ(a)∥1→1, ∥KZ(a)∥∞→∞} ≤ 1 4 . The same row and column estimates hold after the fixed marked derivatives used below. Proof. At the flat field, the selected straight input contributes ϑI. The cross-anchor cancellation can be read directly from the flat forty-nine-path symbol. Its diagonal detour part is proportional to cos kµ 2 Y λ̸=µ cos kλ, so in position space every detour coefficient has an odd displacement in all three transverse directions relative to the selected all-even anchor column. For µ ̸= ν, the factor sin kµ sin kν 2 (2 cos kα cos kβ + cos kα + cos kβ + 2) has an odd µ displacement. Thus no detour term lands on another selected anchor link; the orientation signs are the sine signs in this formula. The flat simultaneous derivative is therefore exactly ϑI. Away from the flat field, every entry is an analytic function of finitely many path holonomies and differs from its flat value by O(g(1 + Rg)). The forty-nine-path tubes have fixed row and column incidence. Shrinking the common weak threshold makes the two Schur sums at most 1/4. Chart-trivialization and every fixed mark add only another finite-incidence O(g(1 + Rg)) term. Theorem 3.3 (Fixed-chart global anchor coordinate). For fixed details Z, the map b BZ is bi-Lipschitz on the convex product chart and a diffeomorphism onto its image. More precisely, ϑ 3 4 ∥a − b∥2 ≤ ∥b BZ(a) − b BZ(b)∥2 ≤ ϑ 5 4 ∥a − b∥2. Every principal subproblem obtained by fixing the exterior anchors has the same lower Lipschitz bound and a volume-independent inverse derivative. Proof. For v = a − b, integrate the derivative along the segment from b to a. Schur’s test gives ∥KZ∥2→2 ≤ 1/4, proving both bounds. The lower bound gives injectivity; the inverse-function theorem gives the smooth inverse. A principal submatrix of KZ has no larger row or column sum, so the same proof applies with exterior anchors fixed. Higher fixed marked derivatives follow by differentiating (I + KZ)−1 and using its Neumann series. Theorem 3.4 (Finite-cell retention and chart charge). There are constants Cret < ∞, cch > 0, and tch > 0, depending only on the fixed cell atlas, such that DQ(U) ≤ CretAQ+ (U) for every fine configuration. Moreover, TQ(U) ≥ tch/4 =⇒ AQ+ (U) ≥ cch. The constants are independent of total volume, ultraviolet depth, periodic wrapping, and the aligned- reflection template. Proof. Use a boundary-rooted tree gauge on the fixed finite contractible cell. By Lemma 3.1, the zero set of AQ+ is precisely the flat set. On that set all common-endpoint paths agree, hence DQ = TQ = 0. Near the flat set, the representation-normalized quadratic comparison and the grounded finite-cell Poincaré inequality give AQ+ ≍ ∥dX∥2 ≍ ∥X∥2 , while smoothness of the finite path atlas and the fact that every retained plaquette word equals the identity on the flat set give DQ + TQ ≤ C∥X∥2 . This proves the retention inequality on one neighborhood of the flat set. Remove a smaller such neighborhood. On the resulting compact set, AQ+ has a strictly positive minimum and the smoothly extended DQ is bounded, so their quotient is bounded there as well. This 11 proves the global displayed inequality without extending a quotient through its zero set. Finally choose tch inside the chart reserve. The closed set {TQ ≥ tch/4} is disjoint from the flat set; compactness and Lemma 3.1 give a positive minimum cch of AQ+ on it. Taking the finite maximum and minimum over the bulk and aligned-reflection templates proves the stated uniformity. Choose smooth decreasing cutoffs γD, γ0, γ1 ∈ [0, 1] with γ0 ≤ γ1 and γD = 1 (DQ ≤ χ2 /4), γD = 0 (DQ ≥ χ2 ), γ0 = 1 (TQ ≤ tch/8), γ0 = 0 (TQ ≥ tch/4), γ1 = 1 (TQ ≤ tch/2), γ1 = 0 (TQ ≥ tch). Define the exact labelled partition χin Q = γDγ0, χcurv Q = (1 − γD)γ0, χcol Q = γ1 − γ0, χout Q = 1 − γ1. Then χin Q + χcurv Q + χcol Q + χout Q = 1 pointwise. The labels are used only inside absolute-value expansions and are recombined before reflection positivity is invoked. Lemma 3.5 (Charged support of every irregular label). After fixing 0 < χ ≤ χ0 with χ2 0 ≤ 4cch, every curvature, collar, or outer label is supported on AQ+ (U) ≥ c0χ2 for one fixed c0 > 0. Every derivative of a splice cutoff through order 2sG is supported on the same charged event and has a fixed-template polynomial bound in χ−1 and the fixed chart thresholds. Proof. On the support of 1 − γD, either DQ ≥ χ2 /4 or a derivative of γD is present. The retention inequality gives AQ+ ≥ χ2 /(4Cret). On the collar and outer supports, or on a derivative of γ0 or γ1, one has TQ ≥ tch/8 after decreasing the fixed thresholds once. The compactness argument in Theorem 3.4 gives a fixed positive action charge, which dominates c0χ2 for χ ≤ χ0. The derivative bounds through order 2sG follow from the fixed smooth cutoffs, Lemma 3.1, and the finite cell atlas; differentiation changes neither transition support nor action charge. Fix R0 larger than the diameter of every local variable support in one normalized step. For each labelled bad block Q, choose a finite integration core IQ containing every variable on which its cutoff and retention estimate depend, and put b HQ = N2R0 (IQ). Join two bad blocks when their b H-sets meet. A bad seed X is a connected component of this graph, with I(X) = [ Q∈X IQ, H(X) = [ Q∈X b HQ, H+ (X) = NR0 (H(X)). For every bad seed X and every configuration in its labelled support, AI(X)(U) ≥ c∗χ2 |X| νov . The statement is uniform under the standing convention. Proof. A color class contains a subfamily S ⊂ X of pairwise disjoint enlarged cells with |S| ≥ |X|/νov. By Lemma 3.5, every selected cell carries c0χ2 of faithful Wilson action. The selected action supports are disjoint, so their charges add. Enlarging the fixed cores once ensures all selected plaquettes lie in I(X). ===== Theorem 1.1 HIT 2 AT 11314 =====
Yang–Mills Existence, Mass Gap, and Nontriviality for Fixed Compact Simple Gauge Groups via Karcher Blocking and Exact Haar–Pivot Matching Harold D. Kirk III1,2,∗ 1 University of Memphis, Memphis, Tennessee 38152, USA 2 ORCID: 0009-0000-6014-8451 August 3, 2026 Abstract Let G be a fixed compact connected simple Lie group and let ρ be a fixed faithful finite-dimensional unitary representation. We construct four-dimensional pure G Yang–Mills theory as a zero-spacing, infinite-volume limit of reflection-positive Wilson lattice measures. The Wilson plaquette function is normalized at the identity to the background quadratic form used to define the coupling u = g2 . A weighted forty-nine-path Karcher block, complete sixteen-alias normal section, charged chart- complement partition, and permanent rooted domain produce a uniform scale-two map on a centered invariant graph, u(2L) = u(L) + 11 CA(G) log 2 24π2 u(L)2 + OG(u(L)3 ). The complete linear block certificate is independent of color and tensors with Ig; all nonlinear constants are finite and may depend on G. At the physical handoff, a group-dependent product Sobolev order 2sG > dG/2 gives a Peter–Weyl Fourier–Wiener landing for the exact one-pivot and multipivot densities. Separated Haar pivots contract nonvacuum compact flux, optical covariance contracts normal-field stretches, and all remaining vertices retain their support charge. A single marked replica–BKAR forest then gives a boundary-to-center two-root connector and one physical clustering rate. General invariant-jet transport, displacement and stress Ward identities, finite dimension-four mixing, and a dimension-six rotation regulator yield a tempered, reflection-positive, translation-continuous, O(4)-covariant Schwinger family. Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction gives a positive-energy Poincaré-covariant local theory with unique vacuum and a strictly positive Hamiltonian gap. Endpoint-safe curvature-source transport produces a nonzero local vector with positive spectral mass in a finite interval above the gap. The theorem concerns the local gauge-invariant curvature-jet observable theory. No estimate is asserted uniformly over the class of compact simple groups. ∗hkirk@memphis.edu 1 Contents 1 The compact-simple-group theorem and proof boundary 3 2 Fixed-group Wilson geometry and Karcher blocking 5 3 Charged chart complement and irregular sector 10 4 Permanent weak renormalization group 23 5 Invariant graph and universal coefficient 39 6 Directed handoff and higher-order Peter–Weyl landing 52 7 Compact boundary-to-center closure 71 8 General invariant jets and Ward identities 72 9 Continuum reconstruction and spectral gap 89 10 Nontriviality and finite spectral window 90 11 Completion for arbitrary fixed compact simple gauge groups 94 A Endpoint packet-shell verification 94 B Root data and representation normalization 109 C The fixed-group Sobolev and Peter–Weyl ledger 110 D Ordinary periodic torons, seams, and wrapping templates 110 E Invariant tensor spaces through engineering dimension six 111 F Theorem-level specialization and dependency map 112 G Representative fixed-group certifications 113 2 1 The compact-simple-group theorem and proof boundary 1.1 Problem and result The constructive Yang–Mills problem asks for a nontrivial quantum gauge field theory on R4 satisfying the Osterwalder–Schrader and Wightman requirements and possessing a strictly positive mass gap [1]. The physical scale, thermodynamic limit, zero lattice spacing, Euclidean covariance, local spectral content, and non-abelian field geometry must be controlled in one compatible construction. Fix throughout a compact connected simple Lie group G, a faithful unitary representation ρ : G → U(Vρ) of dimension dρ, and a positive normalization of the invariant quadratic form for which the adjoint color factor is denoted CA(G). The Wilson plaquette function is wG,ρ(U) = cρ  1 − 1 dρ Re Tr ρ(U)  , where cρ > 0 is chosen once so that the Hessian at the identity is the quadratic form used by the background-field coupling coordinate u = g2 . This choice is a finite linear normalization of the bare Wilson coupling and is part of the fixed-group input register. Theorem 1.1 (Four-dimensional Yang–Mills construction for fixed compact simple G). For every fixed pair (G, ρ) as above, there is a directed sequence of reflection-positive Wilson lattice measures, with microscopic spacing tending to zero and periodic volume tending to infinity, whose local gauge-invariant curvature-jet Schwinger functions converge to a nontrivial Euclidean field theory on R4 . The limiting Schwinger family is tempered, permutation symmetric, reflection positive, translation continuous, exponentially clustering, and O(4) covariant. Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction yields a positive-energy Poincaré-covariant local theory with a unique vacuum and a number m⋆(G) > 0 such that H Ω⊥ ≥ m⋆(G). On the centered invariant weak trajectory, the exact scale-two coupling map is u(2L) = u(L) + 11 CA(G) log 2 24π2 u(L)2 + OG(u(L)3 ), with uniform volume and cutoff control in the permanent weak regime. The reconstructed theory contains a nonzero reflected local curvature vector and positive spectral mass in a finite interval [m⋆(G), M⋆(G)]. 1.2 Fixed-group conventions and finite register Write g = Lie(G) and dG = dim G. All Haar measures are normalized. The finite-volume approximants use the ordinary periodic link space GE(Λ) with periodic gauge transformations; no twisted boundary condition or nontrivial finite-volume bundle sector is summed. This is sufficient for the claimed local theory on the contractible space R4 . The bi-invariant metric, exponential coordinates, Laplacian ∆G, Casimir eigenvalues, and operator norms are those determined by the declared quadratic form. In curvature formulas, Tr(XY ) denotes this normalized invariant bilinear pairing; raw representation traces are written Tr ρ(·). Let sG :=  dG 4  + 1, 2sG > dG 2 . The fixed-group constant register CG consists of the finite quantities used below: the injectivity and convexity radii, a positive local Karcher radius, finite holomorphic BCH and logarithm bounds through order 2sG + 3, the faithful-action Hessian and complement charge, the Peter–Weyl constant, the finite ordinary-periodic toron/seam template register, the adjoint color factor, and the finite invariant-jet dimensions through the retained degree. Every statement called uniform is uniform in cutoff, volume, directed depth, periodic wrapping, admitted exterior histories, and the finite reflected template family, but its constants may depend on CG. Proposition 1.2 (Reflection-positive Wilson input). For every finite periodic lattice Λ and u > 0, in the ordinary periodic link sector, dµG,ρ Λ,u(U) = Z−1 Λ,u exp " −u−1 X p wG,ρ(Up) # Y e dmG(Ue) is gauge invariant and reflection positive for coordinate reflections. Moreover wG,ρ ≥ 0, its zero set is the identity, and on one fixed identity neighborhood cG∥X∥2 ≤ wG,ρ(eX ) ≤ CG∥X∥2 . 3 Proof. The normalized character satisfies Re Tr ρ(U) ≤ dρ, with equality only when ρ(U) = I; faithfulness gives U = e. The Hessian statement follows from the chosen normalization and compactness. Up to a field- independent constant, each plaquette factor is exp{t(χρ +χρ)} with t > 0. Its power series is a nonnegative linear combination of characters of tensor products of ρ and ρ̄, and their irreducible decompositions have nonnegative integer multiplicities. The standard character-expansion reflection-positivity argument therefore applies. 1.3 Proof architecture The proof has four stages. First, the exact Karcher block is placed in a complete full-alias normal chart and combined with a charged chart-complement partition, permanent analytic domain, marked connected logarithm, Wick-stable jet block, and centered invariant graph. The folded spacetime symbol tensors with Ig, while all nonlinear estimates are admitted by the finite register CG. Second, exact background-scheme conjugacy identifies the universal marginal coefficient and a directed schedule reaches the compact matching scale. The higher product Sobolev operator Q p(1−∆p)sG replaces the rank-one order and supplies the fixed-G Fourier–Wiener rows. Third, complete Haar and optical variables are kept in one partition function. A mixed replica– BKAR forest makes every root-to-root path pay separated Haar contraction, optical covariance decay, or pre-existing support charge. Ordinary-periodic toron backgrounds, seams, reflections, and wrapping certificates enter only through the finite template theorem of Appendix D. Finally, a least-label source forest transports the complete invariant-jet system. The degree-four block is group-independent because a compact simple Lie algebra has a unique invariant symmetric bilinear form; possible cubic color tensors first enter the finite dimension-six block. Ward identities, rotation-defect estimates, reflection-positive recombination, and Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction give covariance, the unique vacuum, the gap, and noncollapse. 1.4 External inputs and exact proof boundary Only the theorem-level inputs in Table 1 are imported. The fixed-group geometry, action charge, higher Sobolev landing, periodic-toron template theorem, invariant-jet admission, matching constants, and noncollapse source are internal. Unit-tangent scheme identification and control of the flat complement. 1.5 Self-contained relation to the rank-one construction The companion SU(2) manuscript [14] is a completely expanded rank-one specialization and a useful independent check. It is not a logical input to Theorem 1.1: every common analytic theorem used here is stated and proved in this paper, and every new fixed-group admission is printed below. Appendix F gives a theorem-level specialization map so that a referee can identify which arguments are color-free, which tensor with Ig, and which require new group data. 1.6 Scope The theorem concerns the local gauge-invariant curvature-jet observable sector for each fixed compact connected simple G. It does not assert a bound uniform over groups, a simultaneous large-rank limit, control of every extended Wilson-line or generalized-symmetry sector, or uniqueness of the continuum limit outside the directed construction. Quotient groups are included through their actual compact link group. The construction uses only the ordinary periodic finite-volume sector and retains all flat toron backgrounds occurring in that sector; no claim about a sum over twisted boundary conditions is made or needed. 2 Fixed-group Wilson geometry and Karcher blocking Let Λ be a finite periodic four-dimensional lattice and use the reflection-positive Wilson measure of Proposition 1.2. For each oriented retained link, use the straight two-link path with weight 7/8 and the forty-eight signed-corner staples with weight 1/384 each. On a fixed strongly convex identity tube, define the retained link as their weighted Karcher mean in the bi-invariant metric. Lemma 2.1 (Fixed-group convex Karcher and holomorphic atlas). There are radii 0 < r0,G < r1,G such that the squared-distance functions, product, inverse, exponential, logarithm, and weighted Karcher critical point extend holomorphically to the corresponding local complexification tube. The Karcher Hessian is bounded below there by a positive constant depending only on G. All derivatives through arbitrary prescribed finite order are bounded on the smaller tube. Proof. A compact Lie group with a bi-invariant analytic metric has positive injectivity and strong-convexity radii. The sectional curvature is bounded because G is compact, and the Hessian comparison theorem gives a positive lower Hessian bound on a sufficiently small ball. In a faithful matrix representation the local complexification is an open neighborhood in the complexified group; multiplication, inversion, exponential, and the principal logarithm are holomorphic there. The analytic implicit-function theorem applied to the Karcher critical equation gives the holomorphic mean. Cauchy estimates on nested compact tubes give every fixed derivative order; standard compact Lie-group analytic facts are summarized in [15]. Proposition 2.2 (Exact block covariance and local submersion). The retained map is gauge covariant, orientation covariant, signed-hypercubic covariant, and aligned-reflection covariant. Its linearization at the flat connection has full retained rank. On one fixed weak tube it admits a finite-volume analytic normal section whose matrix kernel and first three marked derivatives are exponentially local, uniformly in volume. Proof. Every path has the same endpoints, and the weighted squared-distance functional is invariant under simultaneous left and right multiplication. Uniqueness of the Karcher minimizer gives gauge and orientation covariance. The signed-corner path family is invariant under the lattice symmetry group. At the flat point, the exact Fourier symbol is the color-free spacetime symbol tensored with Ig and has acoustic value 2I ⊗ Ig and a uniformly gapped normal frame. The full-alias section and normal-fiber contraction are proved in the later normal-chart chapters. Proposition 2.3 (Exact coarea–fiber identity). At finite volume, in the normal fiber chart, dU = J0 det(I + DN P)−1 dW dζ, 5 where the determinant is the ordinary finite-dimensional determinant. The fixed linear determinant is owned once as a scalar normalization, and the field-dependent logarithm is one connected analytic activity. 2.1 Full-alias normal right inverse Theorem 2.4 (Complete folded symbol and flat normal right inverse). Its determinant is det A− 1. 8 2.3 Nonlinear forty-nine-path section Lemma 2.8 (Finite-cell analytic atlas and uniform Cauchy bounds). Let P49 be the finite path atlas for one retained cell and its finite aligned-reflection template family. There exist fixed radii 0 < η0 < η1, uniform under the standing convention, such that the product exponential, multiplication, inversion, logarithm, and Karcher-mean maps defining every template extend holomorphically from Tη0 to Tη1 . Write the canonically rescaled local block as B(x) = Lx + N(x), N(0) = 0, DN(0) = 0. For every exponential kernel rate 0 < m < mP and r = 2, 3, there is a finite constant CN,r(m; η0, η1) < ∞ such that sup x∈Tη0 ∥Dr N(x)∥Km ≤ CN,r(m; η0, η1), uniformly under the standing convention. The same constants, enlarged by a finite maximum, serve the aligned-reflection templates. Proof. For one retained cell the block depends on finitely many g coordinates. The exponential and multiplication maps are entire, while inversion and the principal logarithm are holomorphic on a fixed neighborhood of the identity. At the flat field the Karcher critical point has positive Hessian on the retained tangent space; the analytic implicit-function theorem therefore supplies a unique holomorphic Karcher mean on a fixed complex neighborhood. The path family and the reflected template family are finite, so one may choose common nested radii 0 < η0 < η1 for all of them. On the larger finite-dimensional complex polydisc, every elementary map and their finite compositions are holomorphic and uniformly bounded. Cauchy’s integral estimate on the smaller polydisc gives uniform derivative bounds through order three with denominator powers of η1 − η0. The global block is obtained by translating this fixed finite cell. Each output row meets only a fixed number of input cells, and each input column enters only a fixed number of output cells. Multiplying the finite-cell Cauchy bounds by these fixed incidence numbers gives the two-sided exponentially weighted Schur-kernel bounds. These incidence numbers do not depend on the total volume or preceding weak depth. The aligned-reflection family is finite, so one takes a finite maximum. Proposition 2.9 (Model-specific nonlinear normal section for the forty-nine-path block). Let pm := ∥P0∥Km < 0.546, C2 := CN,2(m; η0, η1), and choose ρm = min  η0 4 , 1 4pmC2  . Thus Φw maps the 2ρm ball into itself and is a contraction. For its fixed point, B(P0T) = LP0T + N(P0T) = T + N(P0T) = w. Analyticity follows from the analytic implicit-function theorem. Differentiation through order three gives finite Bell polynomials in (I + DN(S)P0)−1 , P0, D2 N, D3 N. The inverse norm is below two and the other factors are bounded by Theorem 2.4 and Lemma 2.8. 3 Charged chart complement and irregular sector Put g = √ u and Rg = g−2ε with 0 < ε < 1/6. The symbol χ > 0 denotes the curvature-bad threshold in this section and in the following two irregular-sector sections; the rooted support exponent remains denoted by κ elsewhere. For an enlarged finite tube cell Q+ define the faithful fine Wilson charge and retained coherent deficit by the same representation-normalized plaquette function used in the microscopic measure: AQ+ (U) = X p⊂Q+ wG,ρ(Up), DQ(U) = X p′⊂Q′ wG,ρ(Wp′ ). Let TQ be a fixed smooth invariant dispersion function formed from the squared relative-loop distances of the forty-nine path inputs. It vanishes on the flat set, its small sublevel set lies inside the common Karcher chart, and it uses only one fixed enlarged-cell template. On that sublevel set the retained holonomies Wp′ are defined by the local Karcher atlas. Multiply the displayed retained deficit by a fixed chart cutoff that is one on a prescribed smaller Karcher sublevel and zero before the edge of the larger Karcher tube; this gives a smooth invariant extension to the complete finite-cell configuration space. The threshold tch below is chosen so that the entire curvature-label support lies where this cutoff equals one. Lemma 3.1 (Representation-normalized charge and derivative ledger). For every fixed G, faithful ρ, and integer 0 ≤ r ≤ 2sG + 3, there are finite constants depending only on the fixed cell atlas such that wG,ρ(U) ≥ 0, wG,ρ(U) = 0 ⇐⇒ U = e, cG∥X∥2 ≤ wG,ρ(eX ) ≤ CG∥X∥2 (∥X∥ ≤ r0,G), and ∥DwG,ρ(U)∥ ≤ CGwG,ρ(U)1/2 , ∥Dr wG,ρ(U)∥ ≤ CG,r (2 ≤ r ≤ 2sG + 3). The same bounds, with finite template constants, hold after composition with every Wilson word, retained plaquette word, reflected word, or seam word used below. Proof. Nonnegativity and the zero set are Proposition 1.2. In the chosen invariant metric the Hessian of wG,ρ at the identity is positive definite; Taylor’s theorem gives the two-sided quadratic comparison. The first-derivative estimate follows from that comparison on the identity tube. On its compact complement wG,ρ has a positive minimum while its derivative is bounded, so the same inequality holds globally after enlarging the constant. All higher derivatives are bounded because wG,ρ is a smooth matrix coefficient on compact G. Every word in the fixed path atlas is a finite composition of multiplication and inversion, so the chain rule gives the asserted finite derivative bounds. Lemma 3.2 (Simultaneous selected-anchor derivative). Select the first fine link of every straight two-link anchor as the anchor variable; these links are distinct. Hold every remaining fine link in a detail list Z, and write the simultaneous retained-coordinate map in product exponential charts as b BZ : a 7−→ w. On the common weak chart, D b BZ(a) = ϑ(I + KZ(a)), ϑ = 7 8 , 10 where KZ has fixed propagation range and sup a max{∥KZ(a)∥1→1, ∥KZ(a)∥∞→∞} ≤ 1 4 . The same row and column estimates hold after the fixed marked derivatives used below. Proof. At the flat field, the selected straight input contributes ϑI. The cross-anchor cancellation can be read directly from the flat forty-nine-path symbol. Its diagonal detour part is proportional to cos kµ 2 Y λ̸=µ cos kλ, so in position space every detour coefficient has an odd displacement in all three transverse directions relative to the selected all-even anchor column. For µ ̸= ν, the factor sin kµ sin kν 2 (2 cos kα cos kβ + cos kα + cos kβ + 2) has an odd µ displacement. Thus no detour term lands on another selected anchor link; the orientation signs are the sine signs in this formula. The flat simultaneous derivative is therefore exactly ϑI. Away from the flat field, every entry is an analytic function of finitely many path holonomies and differs from its flat value by O(g(1 + Rg)). The forty-nine-path tubes have fixed row and column incidence. Shrinking the common weak threshold makes the two Schur sums at most 1/4. Chart-trivialization and every fixed mark add only another finite-incidence O(g(1 + Rg)) term. Theorem 3.3 (Fixed-chart global anchor coordinate). For fixed details Z, the map b BZ is bi-Lipschitz on the convex product chart and a diffeomorphism onto its image. More precisely, ϑ 3 4 ∥a − b∥2 ≤ ∥b BZ(a) − b BZ(b)∥2 ≤ ϑ 5 4 ∥a − b∥2. Every principal subproblem obtained by fixing the exterior anchors has the same lower Lipschitz bound and a volume-independent inverse derivative. Proof. For v = a − b, integrate the derivative along the segment from b to a. Schur’s test gives ∥KZ∥2→2 ≤ 1/4, proving both bounds. The lower bound gives injectivity; the inverse-function theorem gives the smooth inverse. A principal submatrix of KZ has no larger row or column sum, so the same proof applies with exterior anchors fixed. Higher fixed marked derivatives follow by differentiating (I + KZ)−1 and using its Neumann series. Theorem 3.4 (Finite-cell retention and chart charge). There are constants Cret < ∞, cch > 0, and tch > 0, depending only on the fixed cell atlas, such that DQ(U) ≤ CretAQ+ (U) for every fine configuration. Moreover, TQ(U) ≥ tch/4 =⇒ AQ+ (U) ≥ cch. The constants are independent of total volume, ultraviolet depth, periodic wrapping, and the aligned- reflection template. Proof. Use a boundary-rooted tree gauge on the fixed finite contractible cell. By Lemma 3.1, the zero set of AQ+ is precisely the flat set. On that set all common-endpoint paths agree, hence DQ = TQ = 0. Near the flat set, the representation-normalized quadratic comparison and the grounded finite-cell Poincaré inequality give AQ+ ≍ ∥dX∥2 ≍ ∥X∥2 , while smoothness of the finite path atlas and the fact that every retained plaquette word equals the identity on the flat set give DQ + TQ ≤ C∥X∥2 . This proves the retention inequality on one neighborhood of the flat set. Remove a smaller such neighborhood. On the resulting compact set, AQ+ has a strictly positive minimum and the smoothly extended DQ is bounded, so their quotient is bounded there as well. This 11 proves the global displayed inequality without extending a quotient through its zero set. Finally choose tch inside the chart reserve. The closed set {TQ ≥ tch/4} is disjoint from the flat set; compactness and Lemma 3.1 give a positive minimum cch of AQ+ on it. Taking the finite maximum and minimum over the bulk and aligned-reflection templates proves the stated uniformity. Choose smooth decreasing cutoffs γD, γ0, γ1 ∈ [0, 1] with γ0 ≤ γ1 and γD = 1 (DQ ≤ χ2 /4), γD = 0 (DQ ≥ χ2 ), γ0 = 1 (TQ ≤ tch/8), γ0 = 0 (TQ ≥ tch/4), γ1 = 1 (TQ ≤ tch/2), γ1 = 0 (TQ ≥ tch). The labels are used only inside absolute-value expansions and are recombined before reflection positivity is invoked. Lemma 3.5 (Charged support of every irregular label). After fixing 0 < χ ≤ χ0 with χ2 0 ≤ 4cch, every curvature, collar, or outer label is supported on AQ+ (U) ≥ c0χ2 for one fixed c0 > 0. m 6.43. The matching forest is four-dimensional: its distinguished root path pays the same optical, Haar-carrier, and reported-support exponents in spatial and Euclidean-time directions. The weighted row and column estimate therefore gives (114) with m0 = mE; fixed endpoint layers are absorbed into CF,G. 74 Exact finite-depth multiscale forest formula For a microscopic depth m, let Rj denote the exact normalized conditional-logarithm map that integrates the scale-j fluctuation field and returns the source- decorated effective interaction at scale j − 1. The maps include the regular/irregular partition of unity before the logarithm is taken, so no all-good denominator occurs. Write Wm(J) = log Zm(J) − log Zm(0) for the normalized finite-volume source logarithm. Iterating the exact maps Rm, . . . , Rjw and then the finite macrobridge is an identity, not an asymptotic expansion. A scale-labelled source forest is generated recursively as follows. A source derivative is a labelled leaf. At one conditional integration, connected cumulant differentiation joins a nonempty partition of the incoming leaves and decorated subtrees to one new vertex labelled by that integration scale. Terms transported without joining retain their existing components. At the terminal matching logarithm, the remaining components are joined by the matching-scale connected cumulants. The ordering of children is fixed by the least source label, so isomorphic terms are not counted more than once. Lemma 8.6 (Exact multiscale Faà di Bruno forest formula). For every finite depth m and every fixed derivative order n, Dn Wm(0)[J1, . . . , Jn] = X τ∈Tm,n Am(τ; J1, . . . , Jn), (115) where Tm,n is the finite family of recursively generated scale-labelled connected trees described above. Each analytic coefficient of each exact conditional logarithm occurs exactly once. After absolute values are taken, the sum is dominated uniformly in m by the rooted weak rows and the finite matching expansion. Hence (115) passes to the infinite weak-depth limit termwise for every fixed n. Proof. For one conditional integration, differentiation of the normalized logarithm is the joint-cumulant identity. The set-partition formula for a derivative of a composition is precisely the Banach-valued Faà di Bruno formula: every block of the partition is one incoming connected component and the outer cumulant joins those blocks at the current scale. Fixing the least-label order makes this representation unique. Induction over the finite sequence of exact conditional integrations proves (115); the terminal matching logarithm is the final induction step. The weak rooted margin pays every nonroot weak vertex by σ < 1, while the finite macrobridge and matching KP expansion pay the finitely many terminal vertices. The one/two-root bounds control a component while its source leaves remain external. These majorants are summable independently of m, so dominated convergence gives the last assertion. For a tree carrying two distinguished source buffers, let v(A, B) be their unique lowest common ancestor. If v(A, B) belongs to the terminal matching logarithm, call the history a matching history. Otherwise its scale label j(v) is the unique weak coarsest-connection scale. Notice that this is a property of the exact recursively generated tree, not a choice made after absolute values or bounds have been applied. Corollary 8.7 (No omission and no double counting). The connected two-buffer part of D2 Wm(0) is the disjoint sum of the matching histories and the weak histories indexed by their unique label j ≥ jw. Contracting all strict descendants of v(A, B) produces a one/two-root decorated vertex whose norm is bounded uniformly by Theorem 7.2 and Corollary 7.4. The decomposition remains exact after regular and irregular sectors are recombined. Every connected tree has exactly one lowest common ancestor for the two source leaves, and the recursive least-label convention gives it exactly one representation in Lemma 8.6. Descendants have strictly finer labels or belong to the same finite conditional vertex and are summed by its connected cumulant. Exact regular/irregular recombination precedes the conditional logarithm at every scale, so the forest formula expands the full measure rather than a conditioned all-good measure. Every connected term has a finite collection of local objects carrying scale labels. Contract every maximal connected collection of finer-scale objects into a decorated root and declare the largest physical scale remaining to be the coarsest scale of the history. Ties are broken lexicographically. Lemma 8.8 (Canonical multiscale allocation). Every connected two-buffer source history is assigned exactly once either to the matching connector or to one weak scale j ≥ jw. Finer decorations attached to its coarsest skeleton have a uniformly summable one/two-root norm. Put dw := CR + m−1 w . 75 For d ≥ dw, X j≥jw Ce−c0| log σ|(d−CR)/ℓj ≤ C′ e−mw(d−CR)/2 . (116) For d < dw the complete history is retained in one fixed enlarged local cluster: it is uniformly bounded for bounded observables and controlled in the local source topology for renormalized fields. Proof. By Corollary 8.7, the lowest common ancestor of the two source buffers assigns every connected tree to exactly one terminal or weak-scale class. This is the first scale in the exact recursive conditional- logarithm formula at which the two source components are joined. The least-label convention removes automorphism multiplicities before estimates are applied. The fixed root algebra sums every finer decoration independently of the number of active nonroot supports. Since ℓj = 2−(j−jw) ℓjw , put x = mw(d − CR)+. The scale sum is bounded by C P r≥0 exp(−2r x). The condition d ≥ dw gives x ≥ 1, and then this is at most C1 exp(−x), proving (116). If d < dw the two buffers lie in one fixed enlarged physical neighborhood. Their ultraviolet contact subtrees are not summed as separate long connectors; they are contracted into the exact local atom described in the statement. Theorem 8.9 (Uniform physical two-source connector). There are dloc, A0, m > 0, independent of resolution, microscopic cutoff, volume, and boundary conditions, with the following properties. (a) For two centered bounded positive-scale observables supported in BR(z) and BR(z′ ), the complete connected majorant is bounded at every separation by Cm(BR(z), BR(z′ )) ≤ Km(z, z′ ) := A0e−m(|z−z′ |1 −2R)+ , (117) and sup z ℓ4 m X z′ Km(z, z′ ) + sup z′ ℓ4 m X z Km(z, z′ ) ≤ K0. (118) (b) If either buffer carries a fixed finite renormalized local source, displacement mark, or rotation mark, then for test sources with disjoint supports at distance d ≥ dloc the connected functional is bounded by Cs,A,Be−md . At shorter distance it is controlled in the local Schwartz/source seminorm supplied by the triangular finite-jet renormalization; no cutoff-uniform pointwise estimate on the diagonal is claimed. The exponent m is common to both classes and to every fixed finite source family. Proof. Combine Lemma 8.5 with the exact forest allocation and the weak scale sum. For d ≥ dloc choose dloc so the variable x = mw(d − CR)+ in Lemma 8.8 is at least one. Weak histories then decay with rate at least mw/2, while matching histories decay with rate at least 1 2 min{mtr, msp}. Hence one may take the common exponent m = m∗ from (111); Lemma 8.2 shows explicitly that this exponent is not degraded by increasing the ultraviolet depth. For bounded observables, the covariance and every short-distance connected local atom are bounded by their sup norms, so enlarging A0 extends the bound to all separations. Lemma 8.1 and integrability of the physical exponential kernel give (118). For a normalized local source, the short-distance cluster is not estimated pointwise. It is exactly the local source coefficient renormalized by the finite triangular counterterm matrix; the finite triangular source theorem printed in Part V bounds it in a finite Schwartz seminorm. Once two source supports are separated, the lowest-common-ancestor edge lies outside both local clusters and the same connector exponent applies. Thus the theorem derives a cutoff-independent physical rate without suppressing or hiding contact terms. All-order source trees Let κm,n(z1, . . . , zn) = Cum(Om,z1 , . . . , Om,zn ). Lemma 8.10 (Labelled-tree extraction). Every connected source-labelled history admits a deterministic allocation to a spanning tree on {1, . . . , n}. An edge joining bounded positive-scale roots is bounded by Theorem 8.9(a). For renormalized local sources, every contact subtree is first contracted into one local renormalized vertex; an edge joining two disjoint vertices is then bounded by Theorem 8.9(b). Only the two endpoint clusters of one edge are retained as roots, and all previously exposed variables are exterior data. 76 Proof. Start with the least label and expose the connected history until it first reaches the least unseen label. Draw that edge, absorb the exposed component into the parent cluster, and repeat. Every new label is attached once. When an edge is estimated, Theorem 6.44 treats the two endpoint clusters as the only distinguished roots and is uniform in all already exposed exterior variables. Theorem 8.11 (All-order physical source-tree bound). For every fixed finite family of centered bounded positive-scale source types there is Cs < ∞ such that |κm,n(z1, . . . , zn)| ≤ n!Cn s X T ∈Tn Y {i,j}∈E(T ) Km(zi, zj), (119) uniformly in resolution, cutoff, volume, and boundary conditions. If the source labels split into two groups with mutually separated supports, the connected cumulant has the additional common factor e−md . Proof. Differentiate the connected source logarithm and first collect each maximal contact subtree into one local root. Apply Lemma 8.10; Theorem 6.44 shows that no matching-stage derivative of order n is required, because each tree edge sees only its two endpoint clusters as distinguished roots. For bounded roots, every edge has the pointwise connector (117); excess attachments are summed by the unused strict KP margin, and the deterministic allocation costs at most the displayed factorial. For renormalized local sources, first contract every contact subtree into its finite local source vertex. The remaining edges join disjoint clusters and carry the separated-support exponential from Theorem 8.9(b). The local vertices are bounded by the triangular source-renormalization seminorms. Repeated Schur summation of the remaining edges, with one Schwartz L1 norm at the tree root, gives (120) and the separated-group exponential. Corollary 8.12 (Uniform source analyticity and exponential clustering). On a fixed neighborhood of the origin, every finite source functional is analytic uniformly in resolution and volume. Thermodynamic and zero-spacing limits of all fixed finite source derivatives exist along one common diagonal subsequence. If two groups of source supports are separated by physical distance d, their connected full moments are bounded by Ce−md with the same m > 0 as in Theorem 8.9. The local source incidence and Lemma 8.3, together with the finite compact matching expansion, give a common analytic source ball. Theorem 8.11 permits termwise differentiation and a countable diagonal extraction. The finite-volume approximants are chosen on a cofinal schedule from Corollary 6.2. The extraction is made once for the countable algebra and a fixed countable set of dyadic physical source scales, and the resulting subsequence is relabelled as the directed sequence appearing in Theorem 1.1; no uniqueness or schedule-independence claim is needed. In every connected partition joining two separated groups, at least one tree edge crosses the separation; factor its exponential and sum all remaining edges with (118). Full moments follow from the set-partition formula. Lemma 8.13 (OS linear-growth condition from the common source ball). For every fixed finite source multiplet there are a finite Schwartz seminorm pN , a radius ρN > 0, and CN < ∞ such that its limiting Schwinger distributions satisfy |Sn(f1, . . . , fn)| ≤ n! Cn N n Y i=1 pN (fi). The constants are compatible under the nested finite source multiplets. In particular the projective Schwinger family satisfies the OS linear-growth condition consumed in SI–5. Proof. Corollary 8.12 supplies one cutoff- and volume-independent analytic ball for the normalized source functional in the Banach norm induced by pN . Apply the one-variable Cauchy estimate to z 7→ W(z P i Ji) and polarize the resulting derivative bound. The common radius gives n!ρ−n N , and the finite incidence constants are absorbed in Cn N . The diagonal limit preserves the inequality, and the nested source construction preserves the constants after enlargement. 77 Lemma 8.14 (Reflection positivity inheritance under exact recombination). Every limiting positive-time quadratic form used in the common Schwinger system is the limit of a reflection-positive quadratic form under the original Wilson measure. Exact normal-chart, coarea, compact-pivot, and source transfor- mations are changes of variables or observable identities, not replacement probability measures, and all regular/irregular and half-cylinder labels are recombined before the OS form is taken. Therefore reflection positivity passes to the common limiting family. Proof. At finite cutoff and aligned reflection volume, let Fi be any finite family of constructed positive-time observables. Pull each Fi back through the exact block, fiber, and source identities to a gauge-invariant function of the original positive-time links. Exact recombination makes this pullback independent of the auxiliary labels. Reflection positivity of the Wilson measure gives X i,j cicj⟨ΘFi Fj⟩a,L ≥ 0. The equality is unchanged by the exact coordinate transformations because all Jacobian and normalization factors remain inside the same normalized integral once. Every entry of the finite matrix converges along the common subsequence, so the closed positive-semidefinite cone contains the limiting matrix. This proves the claim. Theorem 8.15 (Common positive-scale Schwinger system). The common subsequence defines tempered, permutation-symmetric, reflection-positive, continuously translation-covariant Schwinger distributions for the countable algebra generated by the bounded positive-scale observables and every fixed finite normalized local source family. The distributions are projectively compatible and satisfy uniform physical exponential clustering. Proof. The Schwartz estimate gives equicontinuity on each finite tensor power. Diagonal extraction over a countable dense test/source family gives the common projective system. Permutation symmetry passes as an algebraic identity, while Lemma 8.14 gives the complete reflection-positivity mechanism after exact label recombination. Riemann- sum translation errors vanish by the physical Schur bound, Corollary 8.12 gives clustering, and Lemma 8.13 gives the OS linear-growth row. 8.2 Ward identities and Euclidean covariance Theorem 8.16 (Fixed-group invariant-jet admission through dimension six). For a compact simple Lie algebra g: (I1) the invariant symmetric bilinear forms form a one-dimensional space; (I2) there is no reflection-compatible gauge-invariant local vector of engineering dimension at most four; (I3) modulo total differences, equations of motion, and the reflection-odd topological density, the reflection- even scalar dimension-four block is generated by the Yang–Mills density; (I4) the gauge-invariant jet quotient through dimension six is finite. A primitive cubic symmetric color tensor occurs precisely for Lie type Ar with r ≥ 2 and is retained in the dimension-six block; its presence does not enlarge the dimension-four marginal block. The filtered finite-matrix transport and associated-graded contraction used below therefore apply for every fixed G after enlarging only the finite jet dimension. Proof. Simplicity gives the uniqueness of the invariant bilinear form. Semisimplicity makes every finite- dimensional representation traceless on g. Lorentz index counting, gauge covariance, and reflection parity then exclude a single free vector index through dimension four and leave only the quadratic curvature scalar in the even dimension-four sector. Chevalley’s theorem makes the invariant polynomial algebra finitely generated; the degrees are the exponents plus one. Through degree three a primitive symmetric tensor exists only for type Ar, and it first multiplies three curvatures, hence has engineering dimension six. At every fixed engineering cutoff only finitely many covariant derivatives and invariant contractions occur. 8.2.1 Global displacement source An explicit gauge-covariant link source Let Uρ(x) denote the link in a signed coordinate direction ρ. Fix the positive-time profile f and a physical weak scale r, put ur = u(r) and gr = √ ur, and use g rather than u = g2 as the interpolation parameter. The weak-orbit, crossing-cell, pre-owner compact-entrance, and terminal-shell estimates are collected in Theorem 10.4; its single summable majorant controls the functional and its first two derivatives and includes the cell- boundary and no-crossing limits. This proves the first estimate in (170). Exact evenness gives b Q′ r(0) = 0, and integration gives the second. The common-diagonal statement follows from the compatibility argument at the end of this subsection. Lemma 10.3 (Existence of the graph-segment interpolation). After choosing r in the common weak corridor, the segment 0 ≤ g ≤ gr lies in the same real marked domain. At finite cutoff and volume, evaluate the exact interaction at (g2 , R⋆(g2 )) and express its quadratic part relative to the fixed g = 0 grounded Gaussian. For the graph-segment family constructed above, the complete twice-marked endpoint expansion has the following properties. (i) The grounded periodic covariance has volume-independent AA, AF, and FF rows of orders two, three, and four. At first crossing, the fresh marked family lies in a uniform hybrid pivot–jet polydisc and satisfies the original-link Fourier–Wiener incidence row required by the joint Haar–optical collect. (iv) The crossing contribution and the no-crossing terminal contribution are controlled by one geometri- cally summable conditional-packet shell majorant. The same majorant controls the functional and its first two g derivatives, extends to the closed crossing cells, and sends the terminal remainder to zero uniformly as the terminal depth tends to infinity. Consequently the complete normalized two-root functional, with or without one fixed dimension-six defect root, has a cutoff-, volume-, crossing-cell-, and terminal-depth-independent C2 bound on 0 ≤ g ≤ gr. Proof. Appendix A gives the full proof inside this manuscript. Its five subsections establish the grounded- torus covariance and globally subtracted packet division; the weak crossing recurrence and wrapping rows; the twice-marked conditional-packet contraction; the pre-owner hybrid-polydisc Wiener and compact- entrance estimates; and the terminal-shell summation. Compatibility of the physical scale with the common diagonal. Let f be the profile of Lemma 10.1, and let the constants Cf , pf be those in the uniform endpoint and source-defect rows above. Choose the fixed countable source-scale set used in Corollary 8.12 to contain a cofinal dyadic sequence rn ↓ 0. There is an index n such that, for r = rn, Cf  u(r) + r2 L(r)pf  ≤ 1 2 V0(f). Before taking the common diagonal subsequence, enlarge the countable source algebra by the fixed fields Yr(f), ΘYr(f), and their reflected product. The same single diagonal extraction then gives all Schwinger limits entering their OS form and preserves every cutoff- and volume-uniform lower bound. The scale r is not changed after extraction. The cofinal dyadic choice is countable, while u(r) → 0 and r2 L(r)pf → 0 along it. Hence one r satisfies the displayed inequality. Adding finitely many fields at that scale preserves countability of the source algebra. The source-tree theorem and Corollary 8.12 therefore permit the same diagonal argument, and convergence of the finitely many Schwinger functions passes a uniform finite-cutoff lower bound to the limit.
