Let Yr(f) be a fixed positive-time reflected local curvature functional. Suppose along the common diagonal subsequence: (i) every Schwinger function entering Sa,L(ΘYr(f)Yr(f)) converges; (ii) reflection-positive recombination has already been performed; (iii) uniformly in cutoff and volume, Sa,L(ΘYr(f)Yr(f)) ≥ 1 2 V0(f) > 0. Then the limiting Osterwalder–Schrader vector ψY = [Yr(f)] is nonzero and satisfies ∥ψY ∥2 OS = lim Sa,L(ΘYr(f)Yr(f)) ≥ 1 2 V0(f) > 0. 93 Proof. The OS norm of this fixed local polynomial is a finite linear combination of the convergent limiting Schwinger functions. Therefore its finite-cutoff values converge to the limiting quadratic form. The uniform positive lower bound passes to the limit. Corollary 10.7 (Finite positive local spectral window without Wilson pushforward). Let ψY be the nonzero limiting curvature vector from Lemma 10.6. If common physical clustering and the OS spectral argument give supp µY ⊂ [m⋆, ∞), m⋆ > 0, then there exists a finite MY ≥ m⋆ such that µY ([m⋆, MY ]) > 0. No exact Wilson pushforward, Wilson moment identity, or reducing retained Hilbert subspace is used. Proof. The finite spectral measure has total mass 0 < µY ([m⋆, ∞)) = ∥ψY ∥2 OS < ∞. Since [m⋆, n] ↑ [m⋆, ∞), continuity from below implies that some finite n has positive mass. Set MY = n. 11 Completion for arbitrary fixed compact simple gauge groups Theorem 11.1 (Completion of the fixed-group construction). For every fixed compact connected simple G and faithful unitary representation ρ, the group register CG is finite and all hypotheses consumed by Sections 2–10 are satisfied. Consequently Theorem 1.1 holds. Proof. Compactness and the analytic bi-invariant geometry give the Karcher, logarithm, coarea, and faithful-action constants of Section 2. The complete folded block symbol is spacetime-only and tensors with Ig, so the strict right-inverse and optical coercivity constants are unchanged. The chart complement is charged by the faithful Wilson action and the irregular activity is exponentially small. The permanent weak map, centered graph, and BPHZL packet use the same finite-dimensional Lie-algebra tensor calculus, with the universal color factor CA(G). The Peter–Weyl spectral sum converges at the fixed order sG, and the higher hybrid-polydisc argument supplies the one-pivot nuclear and multipivot Fourier–Wiener rows. The ordinary-periodic toron, seam, reflection, and wrapping templates are admitted by Theorem D.3; no twisted sector or finite-volume bundle sum is used. The invariant-jet theorem supplies the complete low-dimensional mixing and dimension-six regulator spaces. The joint Haar–optical forest, source transport, Ward hierarchy, OS reconstruction, and noncollapse theorem then apply with constants depending on G but uniform in cutoff, volume, and directed depth. Appendix F, specifically Table 3, records every independent entry of CG, the first proof call at which its G-dependence enters, and the subsequent consumers. Thus no additional estimate uniform in G is hidden in this completion step: after those finite calls, each remaining mechanism is color-free, tensors with Ig, or takes a finite maximum in a registered finite-dimensional space. Corollary 11.2 (The SU(N) family). For every fixed integer N ≥ 2, the theorem holds for G = SU(N) in the defining representation, and the scale-two map is u(2L) = u(L) + 11N log 2 24π2 u(L)2 + ON (u(L)3 ). No estimate uniform as N → ∞ is asserted. Remark 11.3 (Logical scope). The construction is a fixed-group theorem. The directed subsequence, curvature-jet observable scope, and absence of a large-rank uniformity claim are part of the statement, not omitted conclusions. A Endpoint packet-shell verification This appendix supplies the detailed proof of Theorem 10.4. It is part of the same manuscript and uses the standing uniformity convention of Section 1. The main text isolates the logical endpoint theorem; the statements below retain the full finite-volume, crossing-cell, pre-owner, compact-entrance, and terminal-shell verification needed for that theorem. 94 A.1 Grounded covariance, contact subtraction, and weak admission Lemma A.1 (Grounded-torus curvature covariance). Let C (L) r be the zero-mode-removed transverse covariance remaining after the weak flow is stopped at scale r on a periodic torus of side L ≥ 4r. For j = 0, 1, 2, |∇j C(L) r (x, y)| ≤ Cjr−2−j (1 + dL(x, y)/r)−(2+j) , with constants independent of L. The corresponding AA, AF, and FF rows have decay orders two, three, and four. Proof. Write C (L) r = R ∞ r2 (e−t∆L − Π0)ΠT dt. For t ≤ L2 , periodization gives |K (L) t (x, y) − L−4 | ≤ Ct−2 e−cdL(x,y)2 /t + CL−4 , and derivative rows without the constant term. For t ≥ L2 , use the nonzero spectral gap. The transverse projector commutes with the heat semigroup and has the standard scalar-plus-two-derivative representation. Integration gives the stated rows; the scalar L−4 contribution is O(L−2 ) and is absorbed because dL ≤ 2L. Definition A.2 (Globally subtracted even packet kernels). Let Tab µν(p) be the complete two-open-leg coefficient of the normalized generating functional after all longitudinal, ghost, determinant, normalization, and source contacts have been included. The background Ward identity identifies its exact local contact T(0). Define Tren (p) = T(p) − T(0) before any absolute value is taken. A packet selector is a scalar, color-blind, translation-covariant and reflection-invariant function of the exact conditional scale labels and reported support geometry. Apply such a selector only after the whole BRS/reflection packet and its normalization contacts have been collected. If T = P λ Tλ is the resulting disjoint packet decomposition, absolute convergence gives the exact allocation Tren (p) = X λ Tλ(p) − Tλ(0)  . No cut through an individual packet and no shellwise Ward identity is used. Lemma A.3 (Exact even-contact division of complete packets). For every complete packet class λ, the one-open-leg coefficient vanishes by global color invariance. The renormalized two-open-leg packet is reflection even, vanishes at p = 0, and admits the exact second-order division Tλ(p) − Tλ(0) = X α,β b pα b pβSλ,αβ(p). (188) For every 0 < a′ < a0, the rooted norm of Sλ is bounded by the second support moment of the same complete packet at exponent a0. If the complete packet is represented at weak scale i, the two displayed difference factors carry the canonical relative factor (r/ℓi)2 = 2−2i (189) with respect to the fixed physical source scale r. This factor belongs to the whole packet. It is not assigned to a chosen branch or attachment cut. Proof. The selector is inserted only after the exact normalized packet is formed and is scalar under color, translation and aligned reflection. Hence every selected sum is again a complete packet. Its one-open-leg coefficient is an invariant adjoint vector and therefore vanishes. Global contact subtraction is allocated after the disjoint packet decomposition, so each selected renormalized coefficient vanishes at p = 0. Reflection gives Tλ(−p) = Tλ(p) and therefore removes the linear Taylor term. The integral Taylor formula, or equivalently finite Laurent telescoping in the variables eipα , gives (188). Two momentum derivatives insert two support displacements, absorbed by the unused exponent a0 − a′ . The divided packet has no reflection-even scalar or rank-two local component of engineering dimension four or five. Lemmas 5.22 and 5.23 therefore place its local projection in the stable degree-six block; this observation will be used only for stable membership, not to estimate the nonlocal packet. The scale factor in (189) is instead purely geometric. In the tangent-normalized profile coordinates, one lattice difference at representation scale ℓi = 2i r is (r/ℓi) times the corresponding unit-scale difference. The two explicit factors in (188) therefore contribute exactly (r/ℓi)2 = 2−2i . The two support moments of Sλ are absorbed by the unused rooted exponent. This argument applies to the whole packet, local and nonlocal together. Since the division identity holds for the entire C2 family, zero, one or two g derivatives do not remove the two explicit differences. 95 Lemma A.4 (g-marked one-scale admission). There is g0 > 0 such that, for real 0 ≤ g ≤ g0, the complete normalized interaction and all declared source/defect marks are twice continuously differentiable in the permanent regulator space. An undistinguished non-Gaussian child has parent-normalized row η0(g) ≤ Cg, while a child carrying one or two distinguished g derivatives has row η1(g) + η2(g) ≤ C. The exactly recombined large-field/irregular row and its first two g derivatives extend continuously by zero at g = 0 and are bounded by Cmg−pm e−c/g2 for m ≤ 2. No claim that an attached odd child is O(g2 ) is made. Proof. In the scale-normalized Gaussian coordinate, the cubic Yang–Mills species starts at order g, the quartic and other non-Gaussian even species start at order g2 , and the common species tube controls all field and source marks. Thus an unmarked species row is Cg, while one or two explicit g derivatives remove at most the corresponding powers and give a finite row. The regulator allocations are additive under multiplication, and the Gram and Pruefer estimates in the summed replica–BKAR theorem are insensitive to the parity of an attached tensor. The ordinary parent-normalized child recursion therefore closes for Cg0 below its printed threshold, with two fixed derivative marks handled by Leibniz. The C3 -flat irregular estimate in u = g2 gives the displayed first and second g-derivative rows after the chain rule. In particular the model child gA3 differentiated once by a covariance edge is correctly admitted as an O(g) child, and one g derivative of that child is O(1). Lemma A.5 (Cellwise complete shell decomposition). Fix the finite directed depth M and one crossing cell, including the no-crossing cell. After the selected exact factorization has been completed, apply the canonical reported-support selector only to whole normalized packets. The separated part of the two-root functional has an exact disjoint decomposition b Qsep r,M (g) = X k≥0 FM,k(g) (190) whose coefficients are C2 on the relative interior of that cell. Every normalization, denominator, localiza- tion, irregular and source-motion contact belongs to exactly one selected packet. No assertion that the individual coefficients FM,k agree for two different stopping factorizations is needed or made. Proof. At fixed finite volume and depth, the reporting-telescope and owner-map identities form a disjoint finite or absolutely convergent inventory of complete normalized packets. Their canonical physical reported supports therefore admit disjoint scalar dyadic selectors after packet completion. Inside one crossing cell the stopping depth and exact factorization are fixed, so the graph-segment C2 identity and the finite marked forest permit termwise differentiation. Summing the selectors gives the unselected exact functional, proving (190). Definition A.6 (Finite directed continuation and crossing cells). Fix the physical weak scale r, its endpoint gr = √ ur, and an arbitrary finite further directed depth M. Choose one terminal coarse side Mout and set Mi = 2M−i Mout. The side is first chosen admissible for Lemma 6.1 and is enlarged once, independently of M, to satisfy the fixed-torus smallness threshold of Theorem A.9. For every 0 ≤ g ≤ gr, use the exact weak recurrence u0(g) = g2 , ui+1(g) = Fi(ui(g)), gi(g) = p ui(g), 0 ≤ i < M. Let NM (g) := min{0 ≤ i ≤ M : ui(g) ≥ ū}, with NM (g) = ∞ if the set is empty. Uniform outgoing transversality implies that, when NM (g) < ∞, ui(g) < ū (i < NM (g)), uNM (g)(g) ∈ I⋆. The relative interiors of the sets on which NM (g) = N are called the crossing cells. On such a cell the exact factorization consists of N weak conditionals followed by the printed finite macrobridge and joint compact–optical logarithm. On the no-crossing cell it consists of all M weak conditionals followed by the terminal weak logarithm on the fixed coarse torus. 96 This is a choice of exact factorization, not a modification of the measure. The integer NM (g) is never differentiated. Derivatives are taken inside one crossing cell, where the depth is fixed, and are extended to cell boundaries using the original finite-volume C2 normalized functional. At g = 0 one always uses the no-crossing factorization and later sends M → ∞. Definition A.7 (Canonical finite-volume off-graph coordinates). For the directed schedule in Defini- tion A.6, put εi := e−σMi , δi := δG(Mi), Mi = 2M−i Mout. The first quantity records genuine wrapping histories in the interaction; the second records the scalar massless finite-size comparison. After each complete connected output has been formed and assigned its canonical reported hull, let Pwr i select precisely the history certificates containing at least one periodic wrapping occurrence at some scale not later than i. (191) Thus Ωi = Wi + Bi coefficientwise. Both Ri and R⋆(ui) are written in the same exact schedule-fixed vacuum and tangent-normalized coupling coordinates. No flat charged coefficient is identified with a wrapping history. A.2 Weak recurrence, wrapping control, and crossing cells Lemma A.8 (Full rooted finite-torus stable-map comparison). (196) All constants are uniform in cutoff, original volume and finite directed depth. Proof. Use the complete labelled connected-history representation before forgetting owners. In the scheduled torus and in the rooted thermodynamic step, pair a history whenever its canonically reported hull has an injective periodic lift and contains no charged atlas label. The local Karcher, Gaussian, coarea, determinant, localization, extraction and collect templates are then literally the same, so paired histories and their derivatives cancel coefficientwise. An unpaired regular history has a nontrivial periodic cycle; its reported hull has length at least cMi. Weakening the permanent rooted support exponent once gives Ce−σ0Mi . An unpaired charged history belongs to the exactly recombined U4 row and is Cu−P e−c0/u . For (193), insert H as one distinguished input atom before the connected logarithm. The permanent one-step derivative envelope is a rooted absolute sum with that atom retained once. Vacuum, two-jet and coupling extraction are finite-range linear maps and are paired in the same way. 97 Passing to the adapted norm is harmless: that norm is a fixed finite sum of rooted norms of powers of the rooted-thermodynamic linear map, and each power preserves the preceding paired/unpaired classification. At R = R⋆(u), the free term cancels because both maps are written in the same schedule-fixed vacuum coordinates. The stable tangent uH1 is exponentially local but need not have finite support, so an unpaired periodic image may already be of order εiu. This sharp row gives (192); no quadratic stable cancellation is asserted. If the output is projected to the wrapping ideal, a charged history without a prior periodic certificate must also acquire a new nontrivial periodic cycle. It therefore pays both the charged flat row and εi+1. The same observation applied to a nonwrapping distinguished input proves (194) and (195). These are statements about the full centered interaction map, not inferences from the scalar comparison. Finally differentiate the labelled identities before summing histories. A g mark either stays on the distinguished input or hits one of finitely many local atoms, the scalar orbit, the extraction projector, or a new periodic connector. In the first case the same pairing proves the same-order coefficient in the statement. In every other case the marked child row and the trajectory ledger give the polynomial factor, while the periodic hull or charged event remains present. This proves (196) through two marks. By Lemma 3.29, a history certificate which already contains a periodic occurrence remains periodic after every exact collect, so the old wrapping histories form an output ideal. Let q⋆ < 1 be the full nonlinear rooted-thermodynamic stable Lipschitz constant on the chosen weak ball supplied by Theorem 4.30 and the adapted one-step mean-value contraction following (29). The operator row (193) gives on the finite torus ∥DRΦi(u, R)H∥⋆ ≤ {q⋆ + C(εi + u−P e−c/u )}∥H∥⋆. The remaining nonwrapping difference is not put into the wrapping ideal. The scalar massless finite-size term is already retained in Fi and does not enter the off-graph coordinate. A.3 Twice-marked conditional packets and even-contact contraction Lemma A.10 (Cellwise half-power and two-mark ledger). There are constants Cm and finite exponents pm such that, uniformly in M, in the crossing cell, and in every index before the selected terminal factor, i X h=0 uh(g) ≤ C log(i + 2), i X h=0 |gh(g)| ≤ C √ i + 1, (206) |∂m g ui(g)| + |∂m g gi(g)| ≤ Cm(i + 2)pm , m = 0, 1, 2. (207) The same estimates hold on the no-crossing cell through i = M. Proof. On a cell with crossing depth N, the reciprocal comparison from Theorem 6.4 gives ui(g) ≤ C(N − i + A)−1 before the crossing. On the no-crossing cell the direct fixed-schedule estimate (201) gives the same row with N replaced by M; no extension of the nonautonomous schedule is made. Summation gives (206). 99 For crossing cells use the printed marked C3 orbit row. For the no-crossing cell use (199). In either case every Fi fixes zero, has positive derivative, and can be written as Fi(u) = uϕi(u) with ϕi positive and uniformly C2 . Its finite composition therefore factors as ui(g) = g2 ψi(g2 ) with ψi > 0. Differentiating the exact recurrence twice and using F′ i (ui) ≤ 1 + Cui and |F′′ i (ui)| ≤ C gives polynomial growth in i. Consequently gi(g) = g p ψi(g2) is an odd C2 function and no 1/g singularity occurs at the Gaussian endpoint. The interaction marks required by the finite conditional forest are simultaneously controlled by (203) and (204). All constants are uniform on the compact first-crossing interval and on the enlarged no-crossing weak wedge. Lemma A.11 (Exact finite-depth twice-marked conditional forest). At finite directed depth M, differen- tiate the exact factorization of Definition A.6 with respect to the two curvature roots and at most two copies of g. The result is a finite sum of complete scale-labelled packet trees such that: (i) the two source leaves have one unique lowest common ancestor; (ii) each g mark is owned exactly once by a trajectory coordinate, a conditional logarithm, a source return, the terminal weak logarithm, or the compact handoff logarithm; (iii) every covariance edge, hard-core derivative, field factorial, regulator allocation and normalization contact belongs to one complete one-step packet before multiscale composition; (iv) the coefficients are exactly those of the Banach-valued Faà di Bruno formula. No infinite-depth passage is asserted in this lemma. Proof. Apply the joint-cumulant derivative identity to every exact normalized weak conditional and, on a crossing cell, to the exact finite macrobridge and joint compact–optical logarithm. Then apply the Banach-valued Faà di Bruno formula to the finite composition. This assertion is coefficientwise and uses only finite-volume differentiability; the uniform compact marked bound is supplied later by Theorem A.20. The one-step summed replica–BKAR theorem controls each weak packet, Lemma A.4 controls zero, one and two g marks, and finite source transport controls the external roots. Uniqueness of the lowest common ancestor is the ordinary tree property. Lemma A.12 (Top marked ancestor and complete-packet shell). In a packet tree from Lemma A.11, contract every maximal descendant subtree carrying no distinguished label into its exact parent-normalized decoration, and suppress every maximal unary chain into its exact ordered transfer product. The distin- guished labels are the two curvature roots and the zero, one or two active g marks. They have a unique lowest common ancestor v⊤; let j be its conditional scale. Call v⊤ the top marked ancestor. All vertices strictly above v⊤ are unmarked. The complete coefficient created at v⊤ includes every contracted side decoration with its canonical reported support. Decompose these whole coefficients by their full reported-support shell k using a scalar translation- and reflection-invariant selector. The selected class is therefore a complete BRS/reflection packet with every denominator and normalization contact still inside it. If j ≤ k + C0, the permanent rooted-support row of that whole packet gives C(1 + 2k−j )q e−c2k−j . (208) If j > k + C0, the full support lies inside a fixed enlargement of one scale-j cell and no spatial connector estimate is needed. Proof. Contraction of an unlabelled descendant is its exact conditional value, so it retains its canonical reported support. Suppression of a unary chain is the exact ordered transfer product. After these operations, a tree with at most four distinguished leaves has only a fixed number of essential branching edges, while all arbitrarily long unary portions are already contained in the parity transfer product. The unique lowest common ancestor of all labels is an ordinary tree invariant. If a g derivative lands on a coarser conditional map, its mark is attached at that map and therefore moves v⊤ to that scale. Hence no marked vertex can occur above v⊤. The shell selector is applied only after the full packet at v⊤ has been collected. A remote unmarked side decoration is not cut from its parent and is not asked to carry an external Taylor factor. If exact localization turns its contribution into a smaller-hull contact, that term is already in the finite contact block and is not a shell-k packet. Otherwise its canonical reported support remains part of the whole packet. When that support spans 2k−j scale-j blocks, the strict rooted-support norm and the weak connector theorem give (208), including covariance, hard-core and mixed alternatives. The exact Loc/collect identity of Theorem 5.8 ensures that this dichotomy is made before the stable packet is declared. 100 Theorem A.13 (Complete rooted even-contact contraction). Fix the permanent rooted support exponent and one passive-source order large enough for the two curvature roots. Let ∥·∥⋆ be the adapted rooted marked norm furnished by the macrostep local/nonlocal theorem and the macrostep-to-one-step construction. (211) Thus the strict contraction concerns the entire complete rooted packet; it is not inferred from the finite local jet block alone. Proof. Those results were proved through three active structural marks and arbitrary fixed passive source order, so they apply to the present complete two-root packets and to already formed coupling derivatives. This proves (210) without promoting a finite-dimensional jet estimate to a nonlocal packet estimate. The exact normalized source transfer commutes with translations, global color and aligned reflection. A constant coarse profile pulls back to a constant fine profile. Therefore K(0) = 0 and reflection evenness are preserved, which proves (209). Finally, each displayed lattice profile difference at scale ℓj = 2j r is (r/ℓj) = 2−j times the unit physical difference. Theorem 8.9 (Uniform physical two-source connector). There are dloc, A0, m > 0, independent of resolution, microscopic cutoff, volume, and boundary conditions, with the following properties. The exponent m is common to both classes and to every fixed finite source family. Proof. Combine Lemma 8.5 with the exact forest allocation and the weak scale sum. Weak histories then decay with rate at least mw/2, while matching histories decay with rate at least 1 2 min{mtr, msp}. Lemma 8.1 and integrability of the physical exponential kernel give (118). For a normalized local source, the short-distance cluster is not estimated pointwise. It is exactly the local source coefficient renormalized by the finite triangular counterterm matrix; the finite triangular source theorem printed in Part V bounds it in a finite Schwartz seminorm. Once two source supports are separated, the lowest-common-ancestor edge lies outside both local clusters and the same connector exponent applies. Thus the theorem derives a cutoff-independent physical rate without suppressing or hiding contact terms. All-order source trees Let κm,n(z1, . . . , zn) = Cum(Om,z1 , . . . , Om,zn ). Full moments follow from the set-partition formula. Lemma 8.13 (OS linear-growth condition from the common source ball). Theorem 8.15 (Common positive-scale Schwinger system). The common subsequence defines tempered, permutation-symmetric, reflection-positive, continuously translation-covariant Schwinger distributions for the countable algebra generated by the bounded positive-scale observables and every fixed finite normalized local source family. Riemann- sum translation errors vanish by the physical Schur bound, Corollary 8.12 gives clustering, and Lemma 8.13 gives the OS linear-growth row. 8.2 Ward identities and Euclidean covariance Theorem 8.16 (Fixed-group invariant-jet admission through dimension six). For a compact simple Lie algebra g: (I1) the invariant symmetric bilinear forms form a one-dimensional space; (I2) there is no reflection-compatible gauge-invariant local vector of engineering dimension at most four; (I3) modulo total differences, equations of motion, and the reflection-odd topological density, the reflection- even scalar dimension-four block is generated by the Yang–Mills density; (I4) the gauge-invariant jet quotient through dimension six is finite. A primitive cubic symmetric color tensor occurs precisely for Lie type Ar with r ≥ 2 and is retained in the dimension-six block; its presence does not enlarge the dimension-four marginal block. The filtered finite-matrix transport and associated-graded contraction used below therefore apply for every fixed G after enlarging only the finite jet dimension. Proof. Simplicity gives the uniqueness of the invariant bilinear form. Semisimplicity makes every finite- dimensional representation traceless on g. Lorentz index counting, gauge covariance, and reflection parity then exclude a single free vector index through dimension four and leave only the quadratic curvature scalar in the even dimension-four sector. Chevalley’s theorem makes the invariant polynomial algebra finitely generated; the degrees are the exponents plus one. Through degree three a primitive symmetric tensor exists only for type Ar, and it first multiplies three curvatures, hence has engineering dimension six. A zero-profile-momentum coefficient would be a local, reflection-compatible, gauge-invariant vector of engineering dimension at most four. No such vector exists: a connection mass term is not gauge invariant; Tr dρ(Fµν) = 0 because g is semisimple; traces of dρ(DF) reduce to derivatives of the same zero trace; and two curvature two-forms cannot be contracted with invariant metric tensors while leaving exactly one free vector index. Successive telescoping in the variables zα writes it in the ideal generated by zα − 1 and preserves finite range, proving (148). Theorem 8.9 (Uniform physical two-source connector). There are dloc, A0, m > 0, independent of resolution, microscopic cutoff, volume, and boundary conditions, with the following properties. (a) For two centered bounded positive-scale observables supported in BR(z) and BR(z′ ), the complete connected majorant is bounded at every separation by Cm(BR(z), BR(z′ )) ≤ Km(z, z′ ) := A0e−m(|z−z′ |1 −2R)+ , (117) and sup z ℓ4 m X z′ Km(z, z′ ) + sup z′ ℓ4 m X z Km(z, z′ ) ≤ K0. (118) (b) If either buffer carries a fixed finite renormalized local source, displacement mark, or rotation mark, then for test sources with disjoint supports at distance d ≥ dloc the connected functional is bounded by Cs,A,Be−md . The exponent m is common to both classes and to every fixed finite source family. Proof. Combine Lemma 8.5 with the exact forest allocation and the weak scale sum. For bounded observables, the covariance and every short-distance connected local atom are bounded by their sup norms, so enlarging A0 extends the bound to all separations. Lemma 8.1 and integrability of the physical exponential kernel give (118). For a normalized local source, the short-distance cluster is not estimated pointwise. It is exactly the local source coefficient renormalized by the finite triangular counterterm matrix; the finite triangular source theorem printed in Part V bounds it in a finite Schwartz seminorm. Once two source supports are separated, the lowest-common-ancestor edge lies outside both local clusters and the same connector exponent applies. Every limiting positive-time quadratic form used in the common Schwinger system is the limit of a reflection-positive quadratic form under the original Wilson measure. Theorem 8.15 (Common positive-scale Schwinger system). Diagonal extraction over a countable dense test/source family gives the common projective system. Permutation symmetry passes as an algebraic identity, while Lemma 8.14 gives the complete reflection-positivity mechanism after exact label recombination. Riemann- sum translation errors vanish by the physical Schur bound, Corollary 8.12 gives clustering, and Lemma 8.13 gives the OS linear-growth row. 8.2 Ward identities and Euclidean covariance Theorem 8.16 (Fixed-group invariant-jet admission through dimension six). For a compact simple Lie algebra g: (I1) the invariant symmetric bilinear forms form a one-dimensional space; (I2) there is no reflection-compatible gauge-invariant local vector of engineering dimension at most four; (I3) modulo total differences, equations of motion, and the reflection-odd topological density, the reflection- even scalar dimension-four block is generated by the Yang–Mills density; (I4) the gauge-invariant jet quotient through dimension six is finite. A primitive cubic symmetric color tensor occurs precisely for Lie type Ar with r ≥ 2 and is retained in the dimension-six block; its presence does not enlarge the dimension-four marginal block. The filtered finite-matrix transport and associated-graded contraction used below therefore apply for every fixed G after enlarging only the finite jet dimension. Proof. Simplicity gives the uniqueness of the invariant bilinear form. Semisimplicity makes every finite- dimensional representation traceless on g. Lorentz index counting, gauge covariance, and reflection parity then exclude a single free vector index through dimension four and leave only the quadratic curvature scalar in the even dimension-four sector. Chevalley’s theorem makes the invariant polynomial algebra finitely generated; the degrees are the exponents plus one. Through degree three a primitive symmetric tensor exists only for type Ar, and it first multiplies three curvatures, hence has engineering dimension six. At every fixed engineering cutoff only finitely many covariant derivatives and invariant contractions occur. 8.2.1 Global displacement source An explicit gauge-covariant link source Let Uρ(x) denote the link in a signed coordinate direction ρ. For a positive ν-link e = (x, ν) and µ ̸= ν, put Cµν(x) = 1 2 Ω+µ,ν(x) − Ω−µ,ν(x)  . (123) The minus sign aligns the orientation of the negative-µ plaquette with Fµν. On the full compact link manifold, Xcl a,v is globally smooth, linear in v, finite range, gauge equivariant, signed-hypercubic covariant, and reflection compatible. All group derivatives of its local coefficient through arbitrary fixed order are bounded independently of volume, apart from the explicit canonical factor a−1 in (124). Proof. The projected sine coordinate (122) is a smooth matrix function followed by the fixed equivariant projection Πρ, hence is smooth on the whole group. Under a lattice gauge transformation, a based plaquette is conjugated by the gauge value at its base point; conjugation equivariance of Ωsin gives Cµν(x) 7→ g(x)Cµν(x)g(x)−1 . Consequently Yx,νUν(x) transforms as a tangent vector to the link from x to x + aν̂. Signed permutations reindex the two oriented plaquettes and the profile components, while the orientation sign in (123) is exactly the sign of the transformed two-form. All derivative bounds follow from finite support and compactness of the group. Proof. Write Pρσ = exp ωρσ on a smooth background. By (122), Ωsin(eω ) = (dρ)−1 Πρ[sinh(dρ(ω))] = ω + O(∥ω∥3 ). Table 2 records the standard dimensions and adjoint color factors in the convention CA(SU(N)) = N. For a quotient of the simply connected group the Lie-algebra entries are unchanged. C The fixed-group Sobolev and Peter–Weyl ledger Let λπ be the Laplace eigenvalue of an irreducible representation π and dπ its dimension. By Peter–Weyl and the spectral Weyl law [16], X π∈b G d2 π(1 + λπ)−s < ∞ ⇐⇒ s > dG 2 . Therefore sG = ⌊dG/4⌋ + 1 gives 2sG > dG/2 and ∥f∥A(G) ≤ CG,sG ∥(1 − ∆G)sG f∥2. It does not increase the physical source-mark budget; those marks remain the fixed orders consumed by the boundary-to-center and Ward arguments. In the ordered-multijet realization of Lemma A.17, one Sobolev monomial uses at most eight distinct flow variables at an SU(4) or G2 pivot and at most six at an Sp(2) pivot. Repeated color directions retain distinct variables. Thus, for example, D2 1D2 2 is represented by the ordered word (1, 1, 2, 2) rather than by a fourth derivative along a single flow. D Ordinary periodic torons, seams, and wrapping templates The finite-volume model used throughout is the ordinary periodic product space GE(Λ) with periodic gauge transformations. It is a single, explicit lattice model; no twisted boundary condition and no sum over nontrivial principal-bundle sectors is part of the construction. Since the target spacetime R4 is contractible, this restriction is compatible with the theorem’s local continuum scope. Define the compact set of ordinary periodic flat holonomies TG := {(h1, h2, h3, h4) ∈ G4 : [hµ, hν] = e for all µ, ν}. Lemma D.1 (Tree-gauge reduction of ordinary periodic flat fields). Every flat configuration in the ordinary periodic link model is gauge equivalent to a seam representative whose interior links are the identity and whose four fundamental seam holonomies form an element of TG. Conversely every element of TG defines such a flat periodic configuration. The remaining gauge action is simultaneous conjugation of the four holonomies. 110 Proof. Choose a maximal lattice tree in a fundamental periodic cell and gauge its links to the identity. Flatness makes the holonomy of a path depend only on its homotopy class. The four links closing the fundamental cycles therefore supply holonomies hµ. The commutator loop of two fundamental cycles is a product of plaquette boundaries, so flatness gives [hµ, hν] = e. Reversing this construction gives a flat seam representative from every commuting four-tuple. A gauge transformation at the tree root conjugates all four holonomies simultaneously, and all other gauge freedom has already been fixed. Lemma D.2 (Finite toron and seam atlas). For every fixed compact G, the set TG has a finite cover by ambient product-coordinate neighborhoods on which every block word, Karcher word, coarea factor, Haar factor, reflected word, and source word used in the proof is analytic in the local variables and in the toron parameters. All derivatives through order 2sG + 3 have a finite common bound. The aligned coordinate reflections and seam placements add only a finite template family. Proof. The commutator equations define a closed subset of compact G4 , hence TG is compact. Around each of its points choose ordinary product charts in the ambient manifold G4 ; no quotient chart or classification of singular conjugacy classes is required. In tree gauge, every local factor is a fixed finite composition of multiplication, inversion, the local exponential/logarithm, and the Karcher implicit function, with some arguments left- or right-multiplied by the fixed seam holonomies. These maps are analytic on a sufficiently small product neighborhood and have finite Cauchy bounds through the declared order. Compactness gives a finite subcover. There are four seam directions and a finite aligned-reflection family, so taking a finite maximum gives the common constants. Theorem D.3 (Ordinary-periodic toron and optical admission). The complete full-alias right-inverse, normal-section, optical-coercivity, Haar-minorization, owner, and boundary-to-center estimates hold uniformly over every flat toron background in TG, every admitted seam and aligned-reflection template, and every periodic wrapping certificate. The acoustic toron/background variables remain explicit and are never inverted. No constant uniform over different groups is asserted. Proof. Lift a seam representative to the universal covering lattice. A flat background is gauged to the identity there; deck translation in direction µ acts on Lie-algebra fields by the unitary operator Ad(hµ). Since the hµ commute, these unitary operators are simultaneously diagonalizable over the complexified color space. On each joint color eigenspace the folded block and optical symbols are therefore the identity-background, color-free symbols evaluated at a real quasi-momentum shifted by the corresponding deck phase. The estimates Af (k)Af (k)∗ ⪰ 49 2 I4, ∥P0(k)∥2 ≤ 8 49 , and the printed complex-strip and optical lower bounds hold for every real quasi-momentum. They consequently survive every toron phase without a loss of constant. The retained acoustic variables, including toron tangents, are kept in the exterior/background register; only the same normal optical complement is inverted. Lemma D.2 supplies the nonlinear, coarea, Haar, and marked derivative bounds uniformly over a finite toron/seam cover. On the covering lattice the local gauge trivialization sends every plaquette holonomy to a conjugate of the corresponding fluctuation plaquette. The representation-normalized Wilson function is conjugation invariant, so the charge and its differentiated finite-word ledger are unchanged by this trivialization. The 2sG differentiated bad-core row of Theorem 3.20 is therefore uniform on the same cover. Consequently the complete one-pivot score and Haar minorization retain their printed constants after taking the finite template maximum. On intersections use the fixed least-label owner, so no factor is duplicated. A wrapping certificate records only the four lattice directions, the fixed block range, a finite seam crossing pattern, and an aligned reflection type; there are finitely many such local templates at one normalized step. Their iteration is already controlled by the permanent wrapping ideal rather than by a sum over topological sectors. Taking the finite maximum proves the stated admission and leaves the joint Haar–optical boundary-to-center mechanism unchanged. E Invariant tensor spaces through engineering dimension six The low-dimensional color input can be stated without choosing a basis. The degree-two invariant polynomial is unique for every simple g. A primitive degree-three invariant occurs only for Ar, r ≥ 2. Hence the reflection-even dimension-four scalar block is one-dimensional for every G, while the dimension- six block may contain the additional cubic curvature contraction in type A. The antisymmetric structure 111 tensor is always present through the bracket but contributes only inside the already retained covariant derivative and commutator jets. The manuscript uses the complete finite quotient generated by curvature, covariant derivatives, metric and epsilon contractions, invariant color tensors, total-difference classes, and equation-of-motion contacts through the declared engineering cutoff. No argument relies on a hand-picked SU(2) basis once this quotient is used. F Theorem-level specialization and dependency map The purpose of this appendix is provenance rather than a new estimate. It identifies every independent fixed-group input in the register CG, the first proof call at which that dependence enters, and the later statements that consume it. In particular, the completion proof in Theorem 11.1 does not rely on an unprinted constant or on a rank-one identity. G Representative fixed-group certifications This appendix instantiates the finite ledgers for three non-rank-one groups. For a faithful representation ρ, let Iρ > 0 be its Dynkin index in the declared invariant metric, − Tr dρ(X)dρ(Y )  = Iρ⟨X, Y ⟩. The representation-normalized Wilson function has the expansion wG,ρ(eX ) = cρIρ 2dρ ∥X∥2 + O(∥X∥4 ). The declared background-field convention fixes the positive scalar cρ; any equivalent choice is related by a finite linear change of the bare coupling. 113 SU(4). Use the defining faithful representation on C4 : wG,ρ(U) = cρ  1 − 1 4 Re Tr U  . Then dG = 15, CA(G) = 4, sG = 4, and 2sG = 8 > 15/2. The degree-three invariant is present and is included in the dimension-six jet block. The scale-two coefficient is 11 log 2/(6π2 ). The charge vanishes precisely at I4 and is positive away from it, so Theorem 3.4 applies with the representation-normalized deficit rather than a rank-one raw trace. Sp(2) (USp(4)). Use the standard faithful four-dimensional complex representation: wG,ρ(U) = cρ  1 − 1 4 Re Tr U  . Here dG = 10, CA(G) = 3, sG = 3, and 2sG = 6 > 5. There is no primitive cubic invariant, and the coefficient is 11 log 2/(8π2 ). The same faithful-action and differentiated charged-core ledger applies. G2. Use the faithful seven-dimensional real fundamental representation, viewed unitarily after complexi- fication: wG,ρ(U) = cρ  1 − 1 7 Re Tr ρ(U)  . Then dG = 14, CA(G) = 4, sG = 4, and 2sG = 8 > 7. There is no primitive cubic invariant and the coefficient is 11 log 2/(6π2 ). Faithfulness again makes the identity the unique zero of the charge. For all three examples, the compact ordinary-periodic toron set TG ⊂ G4 is covered by Lemma D.2; Theorem D.3 then supplies the same color-free full-alias and optical constants. The spectral sum in Appendix C converges at the displayed orders, while all nonlinear constants remain finite and group dependent. These are concrete admissions, not hypotheses of the main theorem. Locality separates the ordinary contact terms from pairwise-disjoint supports. Proposition 8.36 (Noncollapse and canonical normalization). The stress Schwinger distribution is nonzero. Its normalization is fixed uniquely by the unit contact coefficient in (160); no independent finite factor is available. Proof. The reflected curvature source of Theorem 10.5 (proved independently in Section 10) defines a nonzero centered local vector. Common physical clustering gives its time-translation correlation a finite positive spectral measure supported away from zero; hence that correlation is not constant and its distributional translation derivative is nonzero for suitable separated test profiles. Result and local-field realization Theorem 8.37 (Stress/Ward construction). The global displacement theorem plus the projective construc- tion above produce a nonzero tempered Euclidean tensor insertion on the bounded geometric Schwinger algebra, with exact translation contacts and signed-hypercubic covariance. The later angular Ward theorem promotes its continuum multiplet to O(4) covariance. 8.2.5 Euclidean rotation covariance Low-dimensional rotation-defect classification. Lemma 8.38 (No low-dimensional gauge-invariant vector). For every compact simple G there is no nonzero local, reflection-compatible, gauge-invariant vector source of engineering dimension at most four, modulo vacuum terms and total derivatives. Proof. A connection mass term is not gauge invariant. At dimension two, Tr dρ(Fµν) = 0 by semisimplicity. At dimension three, traces of dρ(DF) reduce to derivatives of Tr dρ(F) and vanish. A dimension-four gauge-invariant vector cannot be formed from two curvature two-forms using only the metric while leaving one free index; an epsilon tensor would have the wrong reflection parity. Total derivatives are excluded by the stated quotient. 86 Lemma 8.39 (Antisymmetric stress defect begins at dimension six). A local signed-hypercubic, reflection- even, gauge-invariant antisymmetric rank-two source has no component of engineering dimension four or five. Its first possible nonzero continuum jet has dimension at least six. Proof. At dimension four the quadratic curvature tensors are generated by Tr(FµαFνα), δµν Tr F2 , and the corresponding dual-curvature contractions. The first is symmetric; the dual contraction is proportional to a diagonal pseudoscalar tensor; none has an antisymmetric part. Reflection symmetry excludes the odd-derivative dimension-five sector. Corollary 8.40 (Vanishing rotation regulator). In the projective stress Ward identity, the antisymmetric regulator insertion is a2 times a normalized family of dimension-six roots. The marked source bounds therefore make it vanish in the zero-spacing limit. The cutoff rotation Ward identities integrate to full O(4) covariance once the already established translation, reflection, and tensor-source continuity statements are inserted. Stress Ward identity and the antisymmetric regulator source For a compactly supported smooth vector field v, write e0 µν(v) = 1 2 (∂µvν + ∂νvµ) − 1 4 δµν∂· v, aµν(v) = 1 2 (∂µvν − ∂νvµ). The global smooth clover displacement theorem and Haar integration by parts give, after projective source transport, T (e0 (v); O) + Θ  1 4 ∂· v; O  + A(a(v); O) = n X i=1 Sn(O1, . . . , δvOi, . . . , On), (162) where O = (O1, . . . , On) is a finite family of local gauge-invariant source fields. The contact coefficient is one, and for tensor fields δv is the full geometric Lie derivative, including the finite spin action. The first two terms in (162) are the symmetric traceless and scalar metric strains. The third is the regulator response to the antisymmetric part of the lattice displacement. Lemma 8.41 (Vanishing antisymmetric-strain source). For every fixed finite source family and compactly supported antisymmetric profile ωµν , the finite-cutoff antisymmetric insertion has the form Aa(ω) = a2 R(6) a (ω), where R (6) a is a finite sum of dimension-six-and-higher marked local roots. At physical localization scale r, Sc n+1,a Aa(ω), O1,a, . . . , On,a  ≤ Cn,D a r 2 1 + log(r/a) pn,D + rD−4 L(r)pn,D  pN (ω) Y i pN (Oi). (163) Consequently A(ω; O) = 0 in the continuum Ward identity. Proof. Apply the complete filtered normalization of Theorem 8.24 to the rank-two source block. The prescribed principal vector lies in Wst ⊕Wsc and is symmetric in its frame indices. Therefore its contraction with an antisymmetric strain is exactly zero. Any raw hypercubic-only dimension-four coefficient, including the diagonal channel (134), has already been removed through degree D by the complete finite inverse; it is not inferred to vanish from its principal symbol. After this equal-dimensional subtraction, the residual regulator response is the complementary row in Theorem 8.26. Reflection symmetry and centered discretization exclude dimension five, so it is a2 times a dimension-six-and-higher marked root. Transport to physical scale r gives the factor (a/r)2 and at most a fixed logarithmic power. The first omitted finite-jet history gives the second term rD−4 L(r)pn,D . The arbitrary finite marked source tree supplies the remaining seminorms and proves (163). Along the common directed sequence, first a/r → 0 and then r → 0; both displayed terms vanish, so the limiting antisymmetric insertion is zero. Thus the continuum displacement identity is T (e0 (v); O) + Θ  1 4 ∂· v; O  = n X i=1 Sn(O1, . . . , δvOi, . . . , On). (164) 87 Infinitesimal rotations Let ω ∈ so(4) and choose χ ∈ C∞ c (R4 ) with χ = 1 on the unit ball and χ = 0 outside the ball of radius two. Put vR(x) = χ(x/R) ωx. For a fixed finite family of compactly supported insertions, vR(x) = ωx on every support once R is large. The symmetric strain and divergence of vR are supported in the annulus R ≤ |x| ≤ 2R and are uniformly bounded in the corresponding scaled Schwartz seminorms. Lemma 8.42 (Removal of the cutoff annulus). For fixed local insertions O, T (e0 (vR); O) + Θ  1 4 ∂· vR; O  −→ 0 (R → ∞). Proof. The stress and scalar source roots are centered by their vacuum rows. Their supports lie in the cutoff annulus, at distance at least R − CO from the fixed insertions. The all-order physical source-tree theorem gives a common bound CO(1 + R)M e−m(R−CO) for some finite M; the polynomial comes from the scaled cutoff profile and is dominated by the physical exponential. Hence the annulus terms vanish. Theorem 8.43 (Infinitesimal rotation Ward identity). For every ω ∈ so(4) and finite family of local gauge-invariant fields, n X i=1 Sn(O1, . . . , δωOi, . . . , On) = 0, (165) where δω is the combined orbital and tensor-spin rotation derivation. Proof. Apply (164) to vR. For large R, the contacts on the right are exactly the infinitesimal rotations δωOi. Lemma 8.42 sends the left side to zero. Finite source multiplets and integration For every D < ∞, let J≤D be the finite-dimensional renormalized curvature-jet source multiplet. The contact hierarchy preserves the engineering filtration. On J≤D, δω is a finite matrix, allowing same-dimension composite-operator mixing. Lemma 8.44 (Rotation algebra on finite source multiplets). The contact derivations obey [δω, δη] = δ[ω,η] on every J≤D and are compatible under D 7→ D + 2. Proof. At finite cutoff the contact derivations are obtained by differentiating the geometric pullback of test profiles, tensor indices and local source words. Geometric Lie derivatives obey the displayed commutator identity exactly. Finite triangular source renormalization conjugates this finite-dimensional action and therefore preserves the commutator. Dimension-raised regulator contacts vanish by Lemma 8.41; the projective limit and the inclusions of source multiplets preserve the identity. Every finite-dimensional real representation of so(4) integrates to the simply connected group Spin(4). The source multiplets consist of bosonic tensors built from curvature and covariant derivatives, so a 2π rotation acts trivially; equivalently, their geometric tensor action factors through SO(4). Theorem 8.45 (SO(4) covariance of the Schwinger family). Let ρD be the integrated finite-dimensional action on J≤D. Then for every R ∈ SO(4), Sn (ρD(R)v1, Rf1), . . . , (ρD(R)vn, Rfn)  = Sn (v1, f1), . . . , (vn, fn)  . Exact lattice reflection extends the covariance to O(4). Proof. Along a smooth path R(s) in SO(4), differentiate the transformed Schwinger function. The derivative is the left side of (165) with ω = R′ (s)R(s)−1 and therefore vanishes. Lemma 8.44 gives path independence and the group law. Reflection is exact for the Wilson action, block map, clover source and finite source normalization. 88 Osterwalder–Schrader continuation Let Dloc be the local field-polynomial test algebra. The O(4) covariance proved above is an identity of the Euclidean Schwinger system. Rotations preserving the chosen time-zero hyperplane act directly on the positive-time quotient; rotations mixing Euclidean time with space need not preserve that quotient and are not asserted to act unitarily there. Theorem 8.46 (Euclidean covariance and Poincaré reconstruction). The Schwinger family satisfies the full O(4) covariance axiom. Together with reflection positivity, permutation symmetry, temperedness and continuous translations, this gives by the Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction theorem a strongly continuous unitary representation of the proper orthochronous Poincaré group on the reconstructed Hilbert space, with translation spectrum in the closed forward cone. Spatial rotations and translations are obtained directly on the OS quotient; Lorentz boosts arise from analytic continuation of the Euclidean rotation covariance. Proof. Theorem 8.45 is precisely the Euclidean covariance identity required for every finite tensor/source multiplet, and the nested source construction extends it to the complete chosen local field family. The direct OS theorem, Lemma 8.14, and Lemma 8.13 supply the remaining reconstruction hypotheses, including the linear-growth condition. The standard Osterwalder–Schrader covariance reconstruction then produces the positive- energy Poincaré representation. No unitary action of a Euclidean rotation that changes the reflection hyperplane is assumed on the intermediate OS quotient. 9 Continuum reconstruction and spectral gap 9.1 Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction and clustering gap The common Schwinger functional Let D be the countable algebra generated by positive-time translates of the bounded positive-scale cylinders and the normalized finite local source fields constructed by the marked hierarchy. The multiscale source-tree theorem supplies a projective Schwinger functional S on D with the following properties: (S1) temperedness and continuity in every Schwartz test tensor; (S2) stationarity, gauge invariance, permutation symmetry, and reflection positivity; (S3) continuous Euclidean translations; (S4) for every two field polynomials F, G with separated supports, |S(FG) − S(F)S(G)| ≤ CF,Ge−m dist1(supp F,supp G) (166) with one m > 0 independent of the chosen polynomials; (S5) the OS linear-growth estimate of Lemma 8.13 on every finite source multiplet. The constants CF,G may depend on the fixed fields and test functions; the exponent m does not. Theorem 9.1 (Continuous OS reconstruction). Let Θ be time reflection and D+ the positive-time subalgebra. The form ⟨F, G⟩OS = S(ΘF G) is positive semidefinite. Its quotient and completion give a separable Hilbert space H, vacuum Ω = [1], and a strongly continuous self-adjoint contraction semigroup T(t) = e−tH , H ≥ 0. Proof. Reflection positivity gives the positive form and the null ideal N = {F : ∥F∥OS = 0}. Positive-time translations preserve D+ for sufficiently small translations on each compactly supported word and define contractions on the quotient; the usual time-zero limiting argument extends them to all t ≥ 0. For a core vector [F], ∥T(t)[F] − [F]∥ 2 = S(ΘF τ2tF) − 2ℜS(ΘF τtF) + S(ΘFF), which tends to zero by translation continuity. Contractivity and density give strong continuity on H. Reflection symmetry makes the semigroup self-adjoint, and the spectral theorem gives T(t) = e−tH with H ≥ 0. Spectral consequence of one common clustering rate Let D+,0 be the centered positive-time core, D+,0 = {F ∈ D+ : S(F) = 0}. For F ∈ D+,0 let µF be the positive spectral measure of H in the vector [F]. 89 Lemma 9.2 (Core spectral support). For every F ∈ D+,0, supp µF ⊂ [m, ∞). (167) Proof. Let the time support of F lie in a fixed compact interval. For all sufficiently large t, the supports of ΘF and τtF are separated by at least t − cF . Since S(F) = 0, (166) and OS reconstruction give 0 ≤ Z ∞ 0 e−tλ dµF (λ) = ⟨[F], T(t)[F]⟩ ≤ CF e−mt . (168) Fix ε > 0. Then e−(m−ε)t µF ([0, m − ε]) ≤ CF e−mt . Multiplication by e(m−ε)t and t → ∞ gives µF ([0, m − ε]) = 0. Letting ε ↓ 0 proves (167). Theorem 9.3 (Unique vacuum and complete local mass gap). The OS Hamiltonian satisfies H Ω⊥ ≥ m > 0. (169) The vacuum is unique. Proof. The centered classes [F] − S(F)Ω, F ∈ D+, are dense in Ω⊥ . Lemma 9.2 says that the spectral projection EH([0, m − ε]) annihilates this dense set for every ε > 0; hence it vanishes on Ω⊥ . This proves the absence of spectrum in (0, m). For a centered core vector, (168) tends to zero, so its spectral measure has no atom at zero. Density then shows that EH({0})H = CΩ. Thus the vacuum is unique and (169) holds. 10 Nontriviality and finite spectral window 10.1 Endpoint interpolation and reflected noncollapse Let Yr(f) be the tangent-normalized, Wick-centered scalar curvature source at physical scale r, with the source root parallel transported in the normalized finite-jet frame. The test profile f is chosen once at unit scale and dilated with the source so that its dimensionless support remains fixed and lies strictly in positive Euclidean time. Lemma 10.1 (Positive Gaussian OS norm). There is such a profile f for which V0(f) := S0 ΘYr(f) Yr(f)  > 0. The value is independent of r in the normalized frame. Proof. At the Gaussian fixed point the centered scalar curvature composite creates a two-curvature Fock vector. In Fourier variables its squared norm is an integral of the squared symmetrized two-particle amplitude against the positive transverse curvature spectral density. That density is nonzero on an open set, so choosing a positive-time profile whose Fourier transform is nonzero there gives a strictly positive integral. Reflection positivity identifies the integral with the displayed OS form. Scale covariance of the tangent-normalized source makes the value independent of r. 10.2 Uniform endpoint interpolation Theorem 10.2 (Uniform endpoint interpolation master theorem). Fix the positive-time profile f and a physical weak scale r, put ur = u(r) and gr = √ ur, and use g rather than u = g2 as the interpolation parameter. For the complete finite-volume normalized reflected two-root functional, and also with one fixed finite defect root, the following hold on the full segment 0 ≤ g ≤ gr: (i) the graph-segment identities are C2 and exact before every limit; (ii) the stable/wrapping orbit rows, the pre-owner two-mark compact entrance row, and the terminal packet-shell row have constants independent of cutoff, volume, preceding weak depth, crossing cell, and terminal depth M; (iii) differentiation is performed inside each fixed crossing cell before ownership, never on the crossing index, and the resulting total derivatives extend across cell boundaries; (iv) the no-crossing terminal remainder tends to zero through two g-derivatives uniformly as M → ∞. 90 Consequently there is one finite Cf , depending only on the fixed profile and finite source order, such that sup 0≤g≤gr | b Q′′ r (g)| ≤ Cf , sup 0≤g≤gr | b Q′ r(g)| ≤ Cf g, (170) uniformly in all cutoff and volume parameters. The exact graph segment and derivative identity are established immediately below. The weak-orbit, crossing-cell, pre-owner compact-entrance, and terminal-shell estimates are collected in Theorem 10.4; its single summable majorant controls the functional and its first two derivatives and includes the cell- boundary and no-crossing limits. This proves the first estimate in (170). Exact evenness gives b Q′ r(0) = 0, and integration gives the second. The common-diagonal statement follows from the compatibility argument at the end of this subsection. Lemma 10.3 (Existence of the graph-segment interpolation). After choosing r in the common weak corridor, the segment 0 ≤ g ≤ gr lies in the same real marked domain. The grading is read only after each complete BRS/reflection packet is collected. The graph is C3 in u, so g 7→ (g2 , R⋆(g2 )) is C2 at g = 0. The Wilson word, Haar density, normal return, coarea, determinant, and finite Schur endpoint are analytic in the physical field A = ga. After the Gaussian quadratic form is removed, a Wilson term of field degree n carries gn−2 ; species without the g−2 Wilson prefactor carry higher powers. Analyticity under A = ga and the even dependence of the graph coefficients on g give the congruence in (171). Gauge/ghost partners and coordinate Jacobians are collected before the grading is read, so BRS and reflection relations are retained. No C1 statement in the squared coupling u is used. Proof. Appendix A gives the full proof inside this manuscript. Set MY = n. 11 Completion for arbitrary fixed compact simple gauge groups Theorem 11.1 (Completion of the fixed-group construction). For every fixed compact connected simple G and faithful unitary representation ρ, the group register CG is finite and all hypotheses consumed by Sections 2–10 are satisfied. Consequently Theorem 1.1 holds. Proof. Compactness and the analytic bi-invariant geometry give the Karcher, logarithm, coarea, and faithful-action constants of Section 2. The complete folded block symbol is spacetime-only and tensors with Ig, so the strict right-inverse and optical coercivity constants are unchanged. The chart complement is charged by the faithful Wilson action and the irregular activity is exponentially small. The permanent weak map, centered graph, and BPHZL packet use the same finite-dimensional Lie-algebra tensor calculus, with the universal color factor CA(G). The Peter–Weyl spectral sum converges at the fixed order sG, and the higher hybrid-polydisc argument supplies the one-pivot nuclear and multipivot Fourier–Wiener rows. The ordinary-periodic toron, seam, reflection, and wrapping templates are admitted by Theorem D.3; no twisted sector or finite-volume bundle sum is used. The invariant-jet theorem supplies the complete low-dimensional mixing and dimension-six regulator spaces. The joint Haar–optical forest, source transport, Ward hierarchy, OS reconstruction, and noncollapse theorem then apply with constants depending on G but uniform in cutoff, volume, and directed depth. Appendix F, specifically Table 3, records every independent entry of CG, the first proof call at which its G-dependence enters, and the subsequent consumers. Thus no additional estimate uniform in G is hidden in this completion step: after those finite calls, each remaining mechanism is color-free, tensors with Ig, or takes a finite maximum in a registered finite-dimensional space. Scale covariance of the tangent-normalized source makes the value independent of r. 10.2 Uniform endpoint interpolation Theorem 10.2 (Uniform endpoint interpolation master theorem). Fix the positive-time profile f and a physical weak scale r, put ur = u(r) and gr = √ ur, and use g rather than u = g2 as the interpolation parameter. For the complete finite-volume normalized reflected two-root functional, and also with one fixed finite defect root, the following hold on the full segment 0 ≤ g ≤ gr: (i) the graph-segment identities are C2 and exact before every limit; (ii) the stable/wrapping orbit rows, the pre-owner two-mark compact entrance row, and the terminal packet-shell row have constants independent of cutoff, volume, preceding weak depth, crossing cell, and terminal depth M; (iii) differentiation is performed inside each fixed crossing cell before ownership, never on the crossing index, and the resulting total derivatives extend across cell boundaries; (iv) the no-crossing terminal remainder tends to zero through two g-derivatives uniformly as M → ∞. 90 Consequently there is one finite Cf , depending only on the fixed profile and finite source order, such that sup 0≤g≤gr | b Q′′ r (g)| ≤ Cf , sup 0≤g≤gr | b Q′ r(g)| ≤ Cf g, (170) uniformly in all cutoff and volume parameters. The same bound holds after one finite defect root. The fixed dyadic r may then be inserted into the countable source algebra before the single common diagonal extraction. Proof. The exact graph segment and derivative identity are established immediately below. The weak-orbit, crossing-cell, pre-owner compact-entrance, and terminal-shell estimates are collected in Theorem 10.4; its single summable majorant controls the functional and its first two derivatives and includes the cell- boundary and no-crossing limits. This proves the first estimate in (170). Exact evenness gives b Q′ r(0) = 0, and integration gives the second. The common-diagonal statement follows from the compatibility argument at the end of this subsection. Lemma 10.3 (Existence of the graph-segment interpolation). After choosing r in the common weak corridor, the segment 0 ≤ g ≤ gr lies in the same real marked domain. At finite cutoff and volume, evaluate the exact interaction at (g2 , R⋆(g2 )) and express its quadratic part relative to the fixed g = 0 grounded Gaussian. This gives a real C2 family Vg,r with positive finite normalization. In fixed Gaussian coordinates its complete local expansion has the exact grading Vg,r = X n,ν gν Vn,ν;r, ν ≥ max{n − 2, 0}, ν ≡ n (mod 2). (171) Here n is field degree. The Wilson row attains the lower exponent ν = n − 2; Haar, chart, coarea, determinant, and graph-return rows carry additional nonnegative powers. The grading is read only after each complete BRS/reflection packet is collected. The graph is C3 in u, so g 7→ (g2 , R⋆(g2 )) is C2 at g = 0. The Wilson word, Haar density, normal return, coarea, determinant, and finite Schur endpoint are analytic in the physical field A = ga. After the Gaussian quadratic form is removed, a Wilson term of field degree n carries gn−2 ; species without the g−2 Wilson prefactor carry higher powers. Analyticity under A = ga and the even dependence of the graph coefficients on g give the congruence in (171). Gauge/ghost partners and coordinate Jacobians are collected before the grading is read, so BRS and reflection relations are retained. No C1 statement in the squared coupling u is used. Finite-volume Gaussian equivalence and the common analytic tube give positivity, integrability, and uniform C2 control in g. The exact weak block therefore gives the normalized family ⟨F⟩g,r = R F(A)e−Vg,r(A) dγr(A) R e−Vg,r(A)dγr(A) , 0 ≤ g ≤ gr, (172) with V0,r = 0.
