Thus the continuum displacement identity is T (e0 (v); O) + Θ  1 4 ∂· v; O  = n X i=1 Sn(O1, . . . , δvOi, . . . , On). (164) 87 Infinitesimal rotations Let ω ∈ so(4) and choose χ ∈ C∞ c (R4 ) with χ = 1 on the unit ball and χ = 0 outside the ball of radius two. Put vR(x) = χ(x/R) ωx. For a fixed finite family of compactly supported insertions, vR(x) = ωx on every support once R is large. The symmetric strain and divergence of vR are supported in the annulus R ≤ |x| ≤ 2R and are uniformly bounded in the corresponding scaled Schwartz seminorms. Lemma 8.42 (Removal of the cutoff annulus). For fixed local insertions O, T (e0 (vR); O) + Θ  1 4 ∂· vR; O  −→ 0 (R → ∞). Proof. The stress and scalar source roots are centered by their vacuum rows. Their supports lie in the cutoff annulus, at distance at least R − CO from the fixed insertions. The all-order physical source-tree theorem gives a common bound CO(1 + R)M e−m(R−CO) for some finite M; the polynomial comes from the scaled cutoff profile and is dominated by the physical exponential. Hence the annulus terms vanish. Theorem 8.43 (Infinitesimal rotation Ward identity). For every ω ∈ so(4) and finite family of local gauge-invariant fields, n X i=1 Sn(O1, . . . , δωOi, . . . , On) = 0, (165) where δω is the combined orbital and tensor-spin rotation derivation. Proof. Apply (164) to vR. For large R, the contacts on the right are exactly the infinitesimal rotations δωOi. Lemma 8.42 sends the left side to zero. Finite source multiplets and integration For every D < ∞, let J≤D be the finite-dimensional renormalized curvature-jet source multiplet. The contact hierarchy preserves the engineering filtration. On J≤D, δω is a finite matrix, allowing same-dimension composite-operator mixing. Lemma 8.44 (Rotation algebra on finite source multiplets). The contact derivations obey [δω, δη] = δ[ω,η] on every J≤D and are compatible under D 7→ D + 2. Proof. At finite cutoff the contact derivations are obtained by differentiating the geometric pullback of test profiles, tensor indices and local source words. Geometric Lie derivatives obey the displayed commutator identity exactly. Finite triangular source renormalization conjugates this finite-dimensional action and therefore preserves the commutator. Dimension-raised regulator contacts vanish by Lemma 8.41; the projective limit and the inclusions of source multiplets preserve the identity. Every finite-dimensional real representation of so(4) integrates to the simply connected group Spin(4). The source multiplets consist of bosonic tensors built from curvature and covariant derivatives, so a 2π rotation acts trivially; equivalently, their geometric tensor action factors through SO(4). Theorem 8.45 (SO(4) covariance of the Schwinger family). Let ρD be the integrated finite-dimensional action on J≤D. Then for every R ∈ SO(4), Sn (ρD(R)v1, Rf1), . . . , (ρD(R)vn, Rfn)  = Sn (v1, f1), . . . , (vn, fn)  . Exact lattice reflection extends the covariance to O(4). Proof. Along a smooth path R(s) in SO(4), differentiate the transformed Schwinger function. The derivative is the left side of (165) with ω = R′ (s)R(s)−1 and therefore vanishes. Lemma 8.44 gives path independence and the group law. Reflection is exact for the Wilson action, block map, clover source and finite source normalization. 88 Osterwalder–Schrader continuation Let Dloc be the local field-polynomial test algebra. The O(4) covariance proved above is an identity of the Euclidean Schwinger system. Rotations preserving the chosen time-zero hyperplane act directly on the positive-time quotient; rotations mixing Euclidean time with space need not preserve that quotient and are not asserted to act unitarily there. Theorem 8.46 (Euclidean covariance and Poincaré reconstruction). The Schwinger family satisfies the full O(4) covariance axiom. Together with reflection positivity, permutation symmetry, temperedness and continuous translations, this gives by the Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction theorem a strongly continuous unitary representation of the proper orthochronous Poincaré group on the reconstructed Hilbert space, with translation spectrum in the closed forward cone. Spatial rotations and translations are obtained directly on the OS quotient; Lorentz boosts arise from analytic continuation of the Euclidean rotation covariance. Proof. Theorem 8.45 is precisely the Euclidean covariance identity required for every finite tensor/source multiplet, and the nested source construction extends it to the complete chosen local field family. The direct OS theorem, Lemma 8.14, and Lemma 8.13 supply the remaining reconstruction hypotheses, including the linear-growth condition. The standard Osterwalder–Schrader covariance reconstruction then produces the positive- energy Poincaré representation. No unitary action of a Euclidean rotation that changes the reflection hyperplane is assumed on the intermediate OS quotient. 9 Continuum reconstruction and spectral gap 9.1 Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction and clustering gap The common Schwinger functional Let D be the countable algebra generated by positive-time translates of the bounded positive-scale cylinders and the normalized finite local source fields constructed by the marked hierarchy. The multiscale source-tree theorem supplies a projective Schwinger functional S on D with the following properties: (S1) temperedness and continuity in every Schwartz test tensor; (S2) stationarity, gauge invariance, permutation symmetry, and reflection positivity; (S3) continuous Euclidean translations; (S4) for every two field polynomials F, G with separated supports, |S(FG) − S(F)S(G)| ≤ CF,Ge−m dist1(supp F,supp G) (166) with one m > 0 independent of the chosen polynomials; (S5) the OS linear-growth estimate of Lemma 8.13 on every finite source multiplet. The constants CF,G may depend on the fixed fields and test functions; the exponent m does not. Theorem 9.1 (Continuous OS reconstruction). Let Θ be time reflection and D+ the positive-time subalgebra. The form ⟨F, G⟩OS = S(ΘF G) is positive semidefinite. Its quotient and completion give a separable Hilbert space H, vacuum Ω = [1], and a strongly continuous self-adjoint contraction semigroup T(t) = e−tH , H ≥ 0. Proof. Reflection positivity gives the positive form and the null ideal N = {F : ∥F∥OS = 0}. Positive-time translations preserve D+ for sufficiently small translations on each compactly supported word and define contractions on the quotient; the usual time-zero limiting argument extends them to all t ≥ 0. For a core vector [F], ∥T(t)[F] − [F]∥ 2 = S(ΘF τ2tF) − 2ℜS(ΘF τtF) + S(ΘFF), which tends to zero by translation continuity. Contractivity and density give strong continuity on H. Reflection symmetry makes the semigroup self-adjoint, and the spectral theorem gives T(t) = e−tH with H ≥ 0. Spectral consequence of one common clustering rate Let D+,0 be the centered positive-time core, D+,0 = {F ∈ D+ : S(F) = 0}. For F ∈ D+,0 let µF be the positive spectral measure of H in the vector [F]. 89 Lemma 9.2 (Core spectral support). For every F ∈ D+,0, supp µF ⊂ [m, ∞). (167) Proof. Let the time support of F lie in a fixed compact interval. For all sufficiently large t, the supports of ΘF and τtF are separated by at least t − cF . Since S(F) = 0, (166) and OS reconstruction give 0 ≤ Z ∞ 0 e−tλ dµF (λ) = ⟨[F], T(t)[F]⟩ ≤ CF e−mt . (168) Fix ε > 0. Then e−(m−ε)t µF ([0, m − ε]) ≤ CF e−mt . Multiplication by e(m−ε)t and t → ∞ gives µF ([0, m − ε]) = 0. Letting ε ↓ 0 proves (167). Theorem 9.3 (Unique vacuum and complete local mass gap). The OS Hamiltonian satisfies H Ω⊥ ≥ m > 0. (169) The vacuum is unique. Proof. The centered classes [F] − S(F)Ω, F ∈ D+, are dense in Ω⊥ . Lemma 9.2 says that the spectral projection EH([0, m − ε]) annihilates this dense set for every ε > 0; hence it vanishes on Ω⊥ . This proves the absence of spectrum in (0, m). For a centered core vector, (168) tends to zero, so its spectral measure

For a tree carrying two distinguished source buffers, let v(A, B) be their unique lowest common ancestor. If v(A, B) belongs to the terminal matching logarithm, call the history a matching history. Otherwise its scale label j(v) is the unique weak coarsest-connection scale. Notice that this is a property of the exact recursively generated tree, not a choice made after absolute values or bounds have been applied. Corollary 8.7 (No omission and no double counting). The connected two-buffer part of D2 Wm(0) is the disjoint sum of the matching histories and the weak histories indexed by their unique label j ≥ jw. Contracting all strict descendants of v(A, B) produces a one/two-root decorated vertex whose norm is bounded uniformly by Theorem 7.2 and Corollary 7.4. The decomposition remains exact after regular and irregular sectors are recombined. Every connected tree has exactly one lowest common ancestor for the two source leaves, and the recursive least-label convention gives it exactly one representation in Lemma 8.6. Descendants have strictly finer labels or belong to the same finite conditional vertex and are summed by its connected cumulant. Exact regular/irregular recombination precedes the conditional logarithm at every scale, so the forest formula expands the full measure rather than a conditioned all-good measure. Unique-coarsest-scale allocation Differentiate the exact composition of normalized conditional logarithms with respect to source parameters. Every connected term has a finite collection of local objects carrying scale labels. Contract every maximal connected collection of finer-scale objects into a decorated root and declare the largest physical scale remaining to be the coarsest scale of the history. Ties are broken lexicographically. Lemma 8.8 (Canonical multiscale allocation). Every connected two-buffer source history is assigned exactly once either to the matching connector or to one weak scale j ≥ jw. Finer decorations attached to its coarsest skeleton have a uniformly summable one/two-root norm. Put dw := CR + m−1 w . 75 For d ≥ dw, X j≥jw Ce−c0| log σ|(d−CR)/ℓj ≤ C′ e−mw(d−CR)/2 . (116) For d < dw the complete history is retained in one fixed enlarged local cluster: it is uniformly bounded for bounded observables and controlled in the local source topology for renormalized fields. Proof. By Corollary 8.7, the lowest common ancestor of the two source buffers assigns every connected tree to exactly one terminal or weak-scale class. This is the first scale in the exact recursive conditional- logarithm formula at which the two source components are joined. The least-label convention removes automorphism multiplicities before estimates are applied. The fixed root algebra sums every finer decoration independently of the number of active nonroot supports. Since ℓj = 2−(j−jw) ℓjw , put x = mw(d − CR)+. The scale sum is bounded by C P r≥0 exp(−2r x). The condition d ≥ dw gives x ≥ 1, and then this is at most C1 exp(−x), proving (116). If d < dw the two buffers lie in one fixed enlarged physical neighborhood. Their ultraviolet contact subtrees are not summed as separate long connectors; they are contracted into the exact local atom described in the statement. Theorem 8.9 (Uniform physical two-source connector). There are dloc, A0, m > 0, independent of resolution, microscopic cutoff, volume, and boundary conditions, with the following properties. (a) For two centered bounded positive-scale observables supported in BR(z) and BR(z′ ), the complete connected majorant is bounded at every separation by Cm(BR(z), BR(z′ )) ≤ Km(z, z′ ) := A0e−m(|z−z′ |1 −2R)+ , (117) and sup z ℓ4 m X z′ Km(z, z′ ) + sup z′ ℓ4 m X z Km(z, z′ ) ≤ K0. (118) (b) If either buffer carries a fixed finite renormalized local source, displacement mark, or rotation mark, then for test sources with disjoint supports at distance d ≥ dloc the connected functional is bounded by Cs,A,Be−md . At shorter distance it is controlled in the local Schwartz/source seminorm supplied by the triangular finite-jet renormalization; no cutoff-uniform pointwise estimate on the diagonal is claimed. The exponent m is common to both classes and to every fixed finite source family. Proof. Combine Lemma 8.5 with the exact forest allocation and the weak scale sum. For d ≥ dloc choose dloc so the variable x = mw(d − CR)+ in Lemma 8.8 is at least one. Weak histories then decay with rate at least mw/2, while matching histories decay with rate at least 1 2 min{mtr, msp}. Hence one may take the common exponent m = m∗ from (111); Lemma 8.2 shows explicitly that this exponent is not degraded by increasing the ultraviolet depth. For bounded observables, the covariance and every short-distance connected local atom are bounded by their sup norms, so enlarging A0 extends the bound to all separations. Lemma 8.1 and integrability of the physical exponential kernel give (118). For a normalized local source, the short-distance cluster is not estimated pointwise. It is exactly the local source coefficient renormalized by the finite triangular counterterm matrix; the finite triangular source theorem printed in Part V bounds it in a finite Schwartz seminorm. Once two source supports are separated, the lowest-common-ancestor edge lies outside both local clusters and the same connector exponent applies. Thus the theorem derives a cutoff-independent physical rate without suppressing or hiding contact terms. All-order source trees Let κm,n(z1, . . . , zn) = Cum(Om,z1 , . . . , Om,zn ). Lemma 8.10 (Labelled-tree extraction). Every connected source-labelled history admits a deterministic allocation to a spanning tree on {1, . . . , n}. An edge joining bounded positive-scale roots is bounded by Theorem 8.9(a). For renormalized local sources, every contact subtree is first contracted into one local renormalized vertex; an edge joining two disjoint vertices is then bounded by Theorem 8.9(b). Only the two endpoint clusters of one edge are retained as roots, and all previously exposed variables are exterior data. 76 Proof. Start with the least label and expose the connected history until it first reaches the least unseen label. Draw that edge, absorb the exposed component into the parent cluster, and repeat. Every new label is attached once. When an edge is estimated, Theorem 6.44 treats the two endpoint clusters as the only distinguished roots and is uniform in all already exposed exterior variables. Theorem 8.11 (All-order physical source-tree bound). For every fixed finite family of centered bounded positive-scale source types there is Cs < ∞ such that |κm,n(z1, . . . , zn)| ≤ n!Cn s X T ∈Tn Y {i,j}∈E(T ) Km(zi, zj), (119) uniformly in resolution, cutoff, volume, and boundary conditions. For every fixed finite family of renormalized local or marked sources, the corresponding smeared cumulants satisfy |Cum(Φm(f1), . . . , Φm(fn))| ≤ Cn n Y i=1 pN (fi) (120) for one finite Schwartz seminorm pN depending on the source types. If the source labels split into two groups with mutually separated supports, the connected cumulant has the additional common factor e−md . Proof. Differentiate the connected source logarithm and first collect each maximal contact subtree into one local root. Apply Lemma 8.10; Theorem 6.44 shows that no matching-stage derivative of order n is required, because each tree edge sees only its two endpoint clusters as distinguished roots. For bounded roots, every edge has the pointwise connector (117); excess attachments are summed by the unused strict KP margin, and the deterministic allocation costs at most the displayed factorial. For renormalized local sources, first contract every contact subtree into its finite local source vertex. The remaining edges join disjoint clusters and carry the separated-support exponential from Theorem 8.9(b). The local vertices are bounded by the triangular source-renormalization seminorms. Repeated Schur summation of the remaining edges, with one Schwartz L1 norm at the tree root, gives (120) and the separated-group exponential. Corollary 8.12 (Uniform source analyticity and exponential clustering). On a fixed neighborhood of the origin, every finite source functional is analytic uniformly in resolution and volume. Thermodynamic and zero-spacing limits of all fixed finite source derivatives exist along one common diagonal subsequence. If two groups of source supports are separated by physical distance d, their connected full moments are bounded by Ce−md with the same m > 0 as in Theorem 8.9. The local source incidence and Lemma 8.3, together with the finite compact matching expansion, give a common analytic source ball. Theorem 8.11 permits termwise differentiation and a countable diagonal extraction. The finite-volume approximants are chosen on a cofinal schedule from Corollary 6.2. The extraction is made once for the countable algebra and a fixed countable set of dyadic physical source scales, and the resulting subsequence is relabelled as the directed sequence appearing in Theorem 1.1; no uniqueness or schedule-independence claim is needed. In every connected partition joining two separated groups, at least one tree edge crosses the separation; factor its exponential and sum all remaining edges with (118). Full moments follow from the set-partition formula. Lemma 8.13 (OS linear-growth condition from the common source ball). For every fixed finite source multiplet there are a finite Schwartz seminorm pN , a radius ρN > 0, and CN < ∞ such that its limiting Schwinger distributions satisfy |Sn(f1, . . . , fn)| ≤ n! Cn N n Y i=1 pN (fi). The constants are compatible under the nested finite source multiplets. In particular the projective Schwinger family satisfies the OS linear-growth condition consumed in SI–5. Proof. Corollary 8.12 supplies one cutoff- and volume-independent analytic ball for the normalized source functional in the Banach norm induced by pN . Apply the one-variable Cauchy estimate to z 7→ W(z P i Ji) and polarize the resulting derivative bound. The common radius gives n!ρ−n N , and the finite incidence constants are absorbed in Cn N . The diagonal limit preserves the inequality, and the nested source construction preserves the constants after enlargement. 77 Lemma 8.14 (Reflection positivity inheritance under exact recombination). Every limiting positive-time quadratic form used in the common Schwinger system is the limit of a reflection-positive quadratic form under the original Wilson measure. Exact normal-chart, coarea, compact-pivot, and source transfor- mations are changes of variables or observable identities, not replacement probability measures, and all regular/irregular and half-cylinder labels are recombined before the OS form is taken. Therefore reflection positivity passes to the common limiting family. Proof. At finite cutoff and aligned reflection volume, let Fi be any finite family of constructed positive-time observables. Pull each Fi back through the exact block, fiber, and source identities to a gauge-invariant function of the original positive-time links. Exact recombination makes this pullback independent of the auxiliary labels. Reflection positivity of the Wilson measure gives X i,j cicj⟨ΘFi Fj⟩a,L ≥ 0. The equality is unchanged by the exact coordinate transformations because all Jacobian and normalization factors remain inside the same normalized integral once. Every entry of the finite matrix converges along the common subsequence, so the closed positive-semidefinite cone contains the limiting matrix. This proves the claim. Theorem 8.15 (Common positive-scale Schwinger system). The common subsequence defines tempered, permutation-symmetric, reflection-positive, continuously translation-covariant Schwinger distributions for the countable algebra generated by the bounded positive-scale observables and every fixed finite normalized local source family. The distributions are projectively compatible and satisfy uniform physical exponential clustering. Proof. The Schwartz estimate gives equicontinuity on each finite tensor power. Diagonal extraction over a countable dense test/source family gives the common projective system. Permutation symmetry passes as an algebraic identity, while Lemma 8.14 gives the complete reflection-positivity mechanism after exact label recombination. Riemann- sum translation errors vanish by the physical Schur bound, Corollary 8.12 gives clustering, and Lemma 8.13 gives the OS linear-growth row. 8.2 Ward identities and Euclidean covariance Theorem 8.16 (Fixed-group invariant-jet admission through dimension six). For a compact simple Lie algebra g: (I1) the invariant symmetric bilinear forms form a one-dimensional space; (I2) there is no reflection-compatible gauge-invariant local vector of engineering dimension at most four; (I3) modulo total differences, equations of motion, and the reflection-odd topological density, the reflection- even scalar dimension-four block is generated by the Yang–Mills density; (I4) the gauge-invariant jet quotient through dimension six is finite. A primitive cubic symmetric color tensor occurs precisely for Lie type Ar with r ≥ 2 and is retained in the dimension-six block; its presence does not enlarge the dimension-four marginal block. The filtered finite-matrix transport and associated-graded contraction used below therefore apply for every fixed G after enlarging only the finite jet dimension. Proof. Simplicity gives the uniqueness of the invariant bilinear form. Semisimplicity makes every finite- dimensional representation traceless on g. Lorentz index counting, gauge covariance, and reflection parity then exclude a single free vector index through dimension four and leave only the quadratic curvature scalar in the even dimension-four sector. Chevalley’s theorem makes the invariant polynomial algebra finitely generated; the degrees are the exponents plus one. Through degree three a primitive symmetric tensor exists only for type Ar, and it first multiplies three curvatures, hence has engineering dimension six. At every fixed engineering cutoff only finitely many covariant derivatives and invariant contractions occur. 8.2.1 Global displacement source An explicit gauge-covariant link source Let Uρ(x) denote the link in a signed coordinate direction ρ. Ward identity and contact hierarchy For a compactly supported smooth vector field v, let e0 µν(v) = 1 2 (∂µvν + ∂νvµ) − 1 4 δµν∂· v. The scalar strain is represented separately by the coupling-conjugate insertion Θ(1 4 ∂ · v), and the antisymmetric strain by the rotation action. 85 Theorem 8.34 (Exact continuum translation Ward identity). For every compactly supported smooth v and bounded geometric cylinders A1, . . . , An, W(e0 (v); A1, . . . , An) + Θ  1 4 ∂· v; A1, . . . , An  + A(a(v); A1, . . . , An) = n X i=1 S(A1 · · · δvAi · · · An). (160) The coefficient of every contact is exactly one. Repeated differentiation gives the complete contact hierarchy, including the finite tensor-spin Lie derivative for nonscalar insertions. Proof. At finite cutoff, compact-Haar integration by parts for the globally smooth clover vector field gives the exact score identity. The global clover theorem shows that its action on each bounded geometric representative differs from the anchored geometric displacement by O(ℓ2 j ), uniformly after bounded exterior insertions. The right side therefore converges to the geometric derivatives. Theorem 8.33 passes the score decomposition on the left. Exact conditional pushforward and the joint matching transport preserve the score identity by Lemma 8.31. Differentiating the source functional repeatedly distributes the derivation among the inserted observables, so all collision contacts are fixed rather than independently chosen. Corollary 8.35 (Distributional conservation away from contacts).
